The existing Prékopa argument preserves strong convexity when latent variables are integrated out. Raising the marginal density to the \(N\)th power multiplies that modulus by \(N\). Applying the sharp estimate to its actual marginal potential gives


\[
W_2(\Pi_{\lambda,N}^{\theta},\delta_{\theta_{\lambda,\star}})
\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}.
\]


This matches the constant in v3 equation (17) and Lemma A.14. The previous IPLA theorem's \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\) estimate would have been weaker; the new result proves the sharper constant rather than labeling that factor as an error in the paper.




The exact parameter marginal of the lifted Gibbs measure is \(M(\theta)^N\) after normalization. Its negative log potential is \(NF(\theta)\), and the earlier Prékopa argument proves that it is \(Nm\)-strongly convex with minimizer \(\theta_*\). Sharp Gibbs concentration in dimension \(d_\theta\) gives \(\int\|\theta-\theta_*\|^2d\Pi_N\le d_\theta/(Nm)\). The pushforward identity transfers this same moment to the lifted state law before a square root is taken.





The parameter marginal density is proportional to \(e^{-N\Phi}\), so its mode is the actual statistical maximizer and its curvature is \(Nm\). Apply the sharp theorem in dimension \(d_\theta\), obtaining the second moment \(d_\theta/(Nm)\). The exact pushforward identity transfers this same moment to the enlarged target. Taking a nonnegative square root yields the improved equilibrium RMS radius.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine sharp concentration with the actual Gaussian-\allowbreak{}chain law}{Combine sharp concentration with the actual Gaussian-chain law}}


For \(N>0\), \(\gamma>0\), \(\gamma\le2/(m+L)\), Gaussian independent innovations and \(L^2\) initialization, use the scaled ULA transport theorem and the nonexpansive parameter projection. The projected triangle inequality gives

\[\operatorname{RMS}_n\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_N)+\frac{33}{20}\frac Lm\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

The private lemma handles nonempty coordinate sets. The public theorem splits off the empty-coordinate case, where the parameter norm and its error vanish, so no artificial positive-dimension assumption remains.






For any coupling of the current state and Gibbs reference, project the difference with the norm-one parameter map and add the equilibrium parameter error. Minkowski then bounds current RMS by transport plus the sharp radius. Insert the established state-space Gaussian Euler bound. The zero-dimensional parameter case has zero error, and the singleton-state case has zero transport, so the same public inequality covers all independent natural dimensions.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Smoothed-\allowbreak{}model MYIPLA laws and finite-\allowbreak{}time convergence}{Smoothed-model MYIPLA laws and finite-time convergence}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYIPLA-lean}


MYIPLA is exactly Gaussian interacting Langevin for the split-smoothed joint potential \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\). The unsmoothed strongly convex part preserves curvature, while the Moreau residual adds gradient Lipschitz constant \(1/\eta\). The resulting theorem concerns actual finite-moment algorithm laws and their error about the smoothed marginal maximizer.




Let \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\), where \(g_1\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex and \(g_2\) is convex and bounded below. The actual expanded proximal recursion is identified with Gaussian IPLA for \(V_\eta\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Construct the right convexity and smoothness constants}{Construct the right convexity and smoothness constants}}




For convex-combination weights, the envelope contributes a nonpositive convexity deficit and the smooth part contributes the quadratic deficit with modulus \(m\). Their sum therefore has the same strong-convexity modulus. For gradients, subtract at two points and use the triangle inequality on the two component differences, giving \(L_\eta=L_1+1/\eta\). These are joint-space constants; lifting multiplies both by particle count.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify actual laws before using convergence}{Identify actual laws before using convergence}}


Define \(\rho_n=Q\circ Z_n^{-1}\) from the explicitly expanded chain. The pointwise chain identity gives equality of pushforward laws with IPLA for any differentiable envelope having the stated residual gradient. The IPLA recursion is an actual \(L^2\) random variable. Projection by the bounded parameter map and subtraction of a constant preserve \(L^2\), proving finite parameter error about every fixed center without any convergence assumption.




The random variables are first identified by the exact residual-envelope update equality. Their almost-everywhere representative equalities then imply equality of pushforward measures, so the later convergence theorem concerns the actual MYIPLA law. Finite second moments follow from a Lipschitz score acting on \(L^2\) initial data and \(L^2\) innovations; the parameter error about a fixed center remains in \(L^2\) after applying the continuous parameter projection and subtracting a constant. These moment facts justify the ordinary RMS integral in the public bound.





The exact pointwise recursion equality is stronger than a comparison estimate: both descriptions produce the same state for the same inputs. Their distributions therefore agree under any admissible initialization and innovation law. Lipschitz drift growth propagates finite second moments, and parameter projection is bounded. Subtraction of a deterministic center preserves square integrability, ensuring the final parameter RMS is an ordinary finite expectation.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Apply sharp IPLA to a general residual envelope}{Apply sharp IPLA to a general residual envelope}}


The Moreau envelope is convex. Therefore adding it to the \(m\)-strongly convex function \(g_1\) preserves the constant \(m\). Adding their gradient-Lipschitz estimates gives \(L_\lambda=L_1+1/\lambda\). The replicated-pair identity \(\sum_i\|J_i v\|^2=N\|v\|^2\) then yields lifted strong convexity \(Nm\), lifted smoothness \(NL_\lambda\), and lifted dimension \(d_\theta+Nd_x\).



Applying the existing Gaussian ULA result at step \(\gamma/N\) produces the contraction \((1-m\gamma)^n\) and the discretization term


\[
\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\lambda}{m}
 \sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.
\]


The step condition \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_\lambda)\) is the corresponding scaled ULA condition. Initial finite second moments and Gaussian-law independence are explicit. The continuous reference and finite transport quantities are constructed by the shared theorem, so no convergence estimate or invariant measure is an additional MYIPLA hypothesis.



The law-identification helpers also prove that the actual expanded recursion is probability-valued and that its parameter error about every fixed center belongs to \(L^2\). These guards are used before passing from extended norms to ordinary real square-root integrals in the original-center theorem.



With \(\theta_\eta\) a maximizer of the smoothed marginal and \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\) its actual Gibbs reference, the complete statement is

\[\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\eta\|^2\right)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

The generic theorem allows any convex differentiable envelope with the stated residual gradient and \(\eta^{-1}\) gradient constant; the next step derives those properties for the true Moreau envelope.





The sharper equilibrium radius is \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\), while the Euler bias depends on \(L_\eta/m\). The physical step restriction is precisely the lifted restriction after canceling \(N\). Thus increasing particle count reduces equilibrium parameter spread but does not compensate for arbitrarily small smoothing scale in the gradient constant. All terms refer to the continuous smoothed Gibbs reference and the actual discrete MYIPLA law.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Discharge the envelope and moment hypotheses}{Discharge the envelope and moment hypotheses}}


For the actual Moreau envelope, the proximal calculus proves all inputs: convexity, differentiability, \(\nabla g_2^\eta=\eta^{-1}(I-P_\eta)\), and gradient Lipschitz constant \(\eta^{-1}\). Substitute these into the generic smoothed theorem and the finite-moment lemma. The output is the pushforward by an almost everywhere measurable \(L^2\) representative; mapping the whole probability space to the whole state space proves total mass one.






Actual proximal minimization supplies the envelope's convexity, differentiability and residual gradient. Its complement contraction supplies the \(1/\eta\) score constant. Substituting these established facts removes the abstract envelope premises from the general result. Probability mass follows from the measurable pushforward of a probability source, and finite moments follow independently from the recursion, so the numerical convergence bound does not conceal either requirement.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Move the center of a finite projected RMS error}{Move the center of a finite projected RMS error}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProjectionCenter-lean}


Changing a deterministic comparison center costs at most the distance between the two centers in RMS. The proof first works with extended norms, establishes finiteness at the new center, and only then writes ordinary square-root expectations. Probability normalization makes the norm of a constant equal to its Euclidean norm.




For a probability measure \(\mu\) and bounded linear map \(T\), write \(R_\mu(a)=\|T(\cdot)-a\|_{L^2(\mu)}\), initially as an extended nonnegative number. Finiteness is checked before converting this norm to a real number.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Minkowski and finiteness}{Minkowski and finiteness}}


The identity \(Tx-a=(Tx-b)+(b-a)\) and Minkowski yield \(R_\mu(a)\le R_\mu(b)+\|b-a\|\). A constant has that \(L^2\) norm because \(\mu\) has mass one. If \(R_\mu(b)<\infty\), the right side is finite, hence the error about \(a\) is finite too.






For a finite measure of mass \(M\), a constant vector would instead have \(L^2\) norm \(\sqrt M\|b-a\|\). Here \(M=1\), which explains the exact center-distance term. Minkowski is valid before finiteness, and a finite old-center RMS makes its right side finite. Thus existence of the new-center second moment is a consequence of the comparison, rather than an additional assumption.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Pass to ordinary square-\allowbreak{}root integrals}{Pass to ordinary square-root integrals}}


Monotonicity of the extended-to-real conversion is valid once the upper bound is finite. This proves the real RMS inequality. Use the proved finite moments at both centers to identify the norms with \(\sqrt{\int\|Tx-a\|^2d\mu}\) and \(\sqrt{\int\|Tx-b\|^2d\mu}\). The resulting formula cannot hide an infinite moment through the totalized real integral.






Once the two moments are finite, the extended norms agree with the nonnegative square roots of their squared-distance integrals. The same inequality then becomes \(R_\mu(a)\le R_\mu(b)+\|a-b\|\) in real numbers. This order is important because totalized real integration or extended-to-real conversion alone would not distinguish an infinite moment from a valid finite quantity.

\section{\texorpdfstring{MYIPLA about the original nonsmooth marginal maximizer}{MYIPLA about the original nonsmooth marginal maximizer}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Theorem-lean}


The original-center MYIPLA bound combines smoothed-model convergence with a finite uniform potential gap. Integrating that gap gives a gap between marginal potentials, which controls displacement of their actual maximizers by strong convexity. The result keeps the smoothing, equilibrium, transient and discretization terms separate, with no asymptotic Taylor remainder assumed.




Assume \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and \(g_2\) is convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz. Let \(m,\eta,\gamma>0\), \(N>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_1+\eta^{-1})\). Initialization is in \(L^2\), and the actual Gaussian innovations are independent across times and coordinates and independent of initialization.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A finite approximation creates an actual original center}{A finite approximation creates an actual original center}}


The envelope gap gives \(V_\eta\le U\le V_\eta+\eta K^2/2\), where \(U=g_1+g_2\). The smooth strongly convex comparison supplies Gaussian domination and decay of the original marginal \(k_U(\theta)=\int e^{-U(\theta,x)}dx\). Its continuity and vanishing at infinity imply that it attains a positive maximum. Existence of this original center is derived, rather than added as an independent model premise.





The inequalities \(e^{-\eta K^2/2}e^{-V_\eta}\le e^{-U}\le e^{-V_\eta}\) hold on every latent fiber. The smooth comparison has Gaussian tails, so the original marginal is finite, positive and dominated by a decaying Gaussian. Continuity follows by dominated convergence. A positive continuous function decaying at infinity attains a maximum, proving that the original statistical center exists under the primitive assumptions.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Compare the actual marginal centers}{Compare the actual marginal centers}}


For the actual split Moreau approximation, \(\delta=\lambda K^2/2\), so


\[
\|\theta_\star-\theta_{\lambda,\star}\|
\le\sqrt{\frac{\lambda K^2}{m}}.
\]


The original and smoothed marginal maximizers exist under the primitive assumptions. The square-integrable triangle inequality then gives


\[
\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\star\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\le\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_{\lambda,\star}\|^2\right)^{1/2}
 +\|\theta_{\lambda,\star}-\theta_\star\|.
\]


The finite-moment guards are proved before identifying extended norms with real square-root integrals. Substituting the smoothed-model convergence estimate proves the original-center result stated above.



This is deliberately weaker in smoothing order than Proposition A.1's claimed linear expansion. The result requires no Hessian along a parameter fiber and no uniform Taylor domination. The weaker order is a change in the established result, not a silently omitted term.






At their respective minimizing parameters, compare \(F_{V_\eta}(\theta_U)\) with \(F_{V_\eta}(\theta_\eta)\) using the marginal sandwich and minimality of \(\theta_U\). The increase is at most \(\eta K^2/2\). Quadratic growth gives at least \((m/2)\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|^2\). Comparing and taking square roots yields the finite \(O(\sqrt\eta)\) center displacement; a linear-order claim would need additional arguments.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Move the finite error and substitute the proved bound}{Move the finite error and substitute the proved bound}}


Let \(U=g_1+g_2\) on \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x}\). Assume \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\), and has \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient. Assume \(g_2\) is finite-valued, convex, bounded below, and \(K\)-Lipschitz. For \(\lambda>0\), set


\[
V_\lambda=g_1+g_2^\lambda,\qquad L_\lambda=L_1+\lambda^{-1}.
\]


Take \(N>0\), an initial state with finite second moment, independent standard Gaussian innovations at every coordinate and time, independent of initialization, and


\[
0<\gamma\le\frac{2}{m+L_\lambda}.
\]


Write \(\mu_n\) for the law of the actual scaled MYIPLA state and \(\Pi_{\lambda,N}\) for its continuous Gibbs target. Let \(\theta_\star\) maximize the original latent marginal \(\int e^{-U(\theta,x)}dx\). The formal estimate is


\[
\begin{aligned}
\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\star\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\le{}&\sqrt{\frac{\lambda K^2}{m}}
 +\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\
&+(1-m\gamma)^n W_2(\mu_0,\Pi_{\lambda,N})\\
&+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\lambda}{m}
 \sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.
\end{aligned}
\]


The target normalization, finite transport moments, stationary reference, and equilibrium concentration are derived through the existing IPLA/\allowbreak{}ULA development. Neither the displayed convergence bound nor the smoothing bias is an extra assumption. The last term retains its explicit dependence on \(\lambda\); no uniformity in the smoothing scale is asserted. The paper's \(O(\lambda)+O(\lambda^2)\) smoothing expression is replaced by a finite \(O(\sqrt\lambda)\) guarantee, so this is a related proved theorem, not a verbatim reproduction of Theorem 4.1.




First apply the proved MYIPLA bound about \(\theta_\eta\). Its finite second moment makes the center-change inequality valid in real RMS form. Then \[\bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_U\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}\le\bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\eta\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}+\|\theta_\eta-\theta_U\|.\] Substitute the preceding center displacement estimate. Thus smoothing bias, equilibrium concentration and finite-step algorithmic error enter for three different proved reasons; the original nonsmooth target is never assumed to have a Lipschitz score.





Apply Minkowski to \(\theta_n-\theta_U=(\theta_n-\theta_\eta)+(\theta_\eta-\theta_U)\). The first term is controlled by the smoothed algorithm theorem and the second is a deterministic constant with the proved displacement bound. This produces exactly the four displayed contributions. The discretization coefficient still contains \(L_1+1/\eta\), so the statement records the actual tradeoff between smoothing scale and admissible time step.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The nonsmooth single-\allowbreak{}pair energy estimate}{The nonsmooth single-pair energy estimate}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProximalEnergy-lean}


The energy inequality compares the true proximal output with any comparator while retaining the original nonsmooth potential difference at the input. An input subgradient controls the difference between input and proximal output. Completing a square produces a forcing term proportional to the square of the proximal scale, the correct quantity for summing particle energy estimates.




For convex \(f\), a true proximal minimizer \(p=P_\eta(x)\), positive \(\eta\), and a genuine input subgradient \(s\in\partial f(x)\), proximity controls both squared distance and the original nonsmooth energy.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Expand supports and complete a square}{Expand supports and complete a square}}


Let \(p=P_\lambda(y)\), \(s\in\partial g_2(y)\), and \(a\) be any comparator. Proximal optimality supplies the residual subgradient at \(p\). Combine its support inequality at \(a\) with the support inequality for \(s\) at \(p\), expand the squared distances, and use the nonnegative square \(\|(y-p)-\lambda s\|^2\). The result is v3 Lemma A.8:


\[
\|p-a\|^2\le\|y-a\|^2
 -2\lambda\bigl(g_2(y)-g_2(a)\bigr)+\lambda^2\|s\|^2.
\]


If \(\|s\|^2\le C\), replace the last term by \(\lambda^2C\). The proof works with any genuine subgradient satisfying that bound; it does not assume an energy estimate or a convergence statement. Taking the minimal section in C1 is one way to supply such a vector.





Write \(d=x-p\). The residual support at \(p\) implies \(2\eta[f(p)-f(a)]\le2\langle d,p-a\rangle\). The input support gives \(f(x)-f(p)\le\langle s,d\rangle\). Add these to the squared-distance expansion. The remaining scalar expression is \(2\eta\langle s,d\rangle-\|d\|^2\le\eta^2\|s\|^2\), obtained from \(\|d-\eta s\|^2\ge0\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Instantiate the chosen point and bound the forcing}{Instantiate the chosen point and bound the forcing}}


The existence theorem proves that the chosen \(P_\eta(x)\) meets the variational premise. Substitution gives \(\|P_\eta(x)-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\eta(f(x)-f(a))+\eta^2\|s\|^2\). If \(\|s\|^2\le C\), multiply by the nonnegative \(\eta^2\) to replace the final term by \(\eta^2 C\). The vector must satisfy the true support inequality; a merely named subgradient is insufficient.




Expand \(\|p-y\|^2=\|x-y\|^2-2\langle x-p,x-y\rangle+\|x-p\|^2\), and use the residual subgradient at \(p\) to replace its pairing with the potential difference. A supplied subgradient \(s_x\in\partial f(x)\) bounds the remaining difference \(f(x)-f(p)\) by \(\langle s_x,x-p\rangle\). Completing the square gives \(2\lambda\langle s_x,x-p\rangle-\|x-p\|^2\le\lambda^2\|s_x\|^2\). Thus a bound \(\|s_x\|^2\le C\) produces the forcing \(\lambda^2C\). This algebra is why that forcing has a squared proximal scale.





The comparator is arbitrary, so the same deterministic inequality can later be applied with a random reference point pointwise. A bound on \(\|s\|^2\) supplies only the last forcing term; it does not replace the requirement that \(s\) be a genuine supporting subgradient. The positive scale ensures both actual minimizer existence and validity of the residual optimality characterization used in the argument.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Jensen repairs the averaged pairwise energy}{Jensen repairs the averaged pairwise energy}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ParticleEnergy-lean}


The common parameter is averaged after independent pairwise proximal calculations. This averaging is generally different from minimizing one shared-state proximal objective. The valid lifted energy estimate is obtained by Jensen's inequality for the parameter output, exact scaling of latent output squares, and a sum of genuine single-pair energy inequalities.


\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Averaging squares in the shared parameter}{Averaging squares in the shared parameter}}


Cauchy–Schwarz for finite sums gives \(N(N^{-1}\sum_i b_i)^2\le\sum_i b_i^2\). Apply it separately to each parameter coordinate of the pairwise outputs. Each latent block instead satisfies an exact equality after multiplying by \(N\), since \((\sqrt N)^2=N\). Summing all coordinates yields

\[\|\operatorname{Pair}_P(z)-a\|^2\le\frac1N\sum_i\|P(J_i z)-J_i a\|^2.\]
\[N\left|\frac1N\sum_i p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}\right|^2\le\sum_i|p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}|^2.\]

For particle coordinate \((i,j)\), unscaling instead gives the exact identity

\[N|N^{-1/2}p_{i,x,j}-a_{i,j}|^2=|p_{i,x,j}-\sqrt N a_{i,j}|^2.\]



For vectors \(b_i\), Jensen says \(\|N^{-1}\sum_i b_i\|^2\le N^{-1}\sum_i\|b_i\|^2\). Here \(b_i\) is the parameter component of the \(i\)-th output minus the common comparator parameter. Each latent component instead contributes exactly \(N^{-1}\|p_{i,x}-\sqrt N a_i\|^2\). Adding both blocks gives the displayed inequality for completely arbitrary pair maps, independently of their convex or proximal properties.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Sum genuine proximal energy inequalities}{Sum genuine proximal energy inequalities}}


The roadmap on v3 page 19 identifies averaged pairwise proximity with the proximal map of the lifted averaged potential. Shared parameter coordinates prevent that identity in general. The needed energy conclusion can nevertheless be proved without changing the algorithm.



For arbitrary pair outputs, Jensen's inequality for their averaged parameter gives


\[
\|\operatorname{Pair}_P(z)-a\|^2
\le\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\|P(J_i z)-J_i a\|^2.
\]


The scaled latent-coordinate contribution is an exact identity; only the parameter contribution requires an inequality. Apply the single-pair proximal energy result to every summand. Using \(\sum_i\|J_i(z-a)\|^2=N\|z-a\|^2\) and \(G_2=N^{-1}\sum_i g_2\circ J_i\) gives


\[
\|\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\lambda}(z)-a\|^2
\le\|z-a\|^2-2\lambda\bigl(G_2(z)-G_2(a)\bigr)+\lambda^2C.
\]


This lifted energy inequality is fully proved for actual minimizers. It repairs the deterministic proof bridge needed in Corollary A.13 while preserving the paper's particle algorithm and its original nonsmooth energy. It is still only one part of the full stochastic convergence proof.



The failure of the lifted-proximal identity has a concrete Lipschitz witness. Take \(N=2\), \(g_2(\theta,x)=|\theta-x|\), smoothing one, common input parameter \(1\) and physical particles \((0,4)\). The genuine pairwise proximal points are \((1/2,1/2)\) and \((2,3)\). Averaging only their parameter components gives \((5/4,1/2,3)\). In the lifted proximal objective with its scaled particle norm, the feasible point \((1,1,3)\) has value \(3/2\), while this pairwise output has value \(51/32\). The strict inequality is kernel checked below. The averaged energy proof above repairs the necessary estimate without this false identity.




Apply the preceding true proximal energy inequality separately at \(A_i z\), comparing to \(A_i y\). Sum all \(N\) inequalities, and use \(\sum_i\|A_i(z-y)\|^2=N\|z-y\|^2\). Jensen's inequality bounds the squared norm of the averaged parameter output, while the scaled latent output gives exact squared terms. After division by positive \(N\), the energy difference is \(N^{-1}\sum_i[f(A_i z)-f(A_i y)]\), and the remainder is the average of the actual squared subgradient bounds. This establishes the inequality for the implemented pairwise map without identifying it with a single proximal map on the shared state.





After applying the single-pair inequality, the initial squared distances sum to \(N\|z-a\|^2\). The potential differences sum to \(N[G_2(z)-G_2(a)]\), and the squared subgradient bounds average to the stated forcing constant. Dividing by \(N>0\) therefore gives the required state estimate. The argument uses only a comparison inequality for the actual averaged output; it does not claim that this output solves a shared-parameter minimization problem.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Reference drift moments uniform over smoothing scales}{Reference drift moments uniform over smoothing scales}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingMoments-lean}


The splitting reference requires a bound on its total drift magnitude that remains finite as the envelope scale decreases. Value Lipschitzness bounds the envelope gradient uniformly, while strong convexity and sharp Gibbs concentration control the smooth score relative to a true mode. This avoids using the envelope's diverging gradient Lipschitz constant in the numerical moment bound.




For \(F=f+e\), assume \(F\) is \(m\)-strongly convex, \(f,e\) differentiable, \(\nabla f\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, and \(\|\nabla e\|\le K\). The goal is a Gibbs drift bound using \(L,m,K\), without an inverse smoothing scale.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The envelope preserves a global value Lipschitz constant}{The envelope preserves a global value Lipschitz constant}}


Let \(p=P_\lambda(y)\). As a competitor at \(x\), use \(p+x-y\), so its quadratic penalty equals that of \(p\) at \(y\). The \(K\)-Lipschitz inequality for \(f\) changes the function value by at most \(K\|x-y\|\). Thus \(f^\lambda(x)\le f^\lambda(y)+K\|x-y\|\), and reversing \(x,y\) proves the same global Lipschitz constant.




Choose the actual minimizer \(p=P_\lambda(y)\), and test the objective at \(x\) with \(p+(x-y)\). The two quadratic penalties agree because \(p+(x-y)-x=p-y\); only the potential value changes, by at most \(K\|x-y\|\). Therefore \(f^\lambda(x)\le f^\lambda(y)+K\|x-y\|\). Reversing the inputs gives the absolute-value bound. This competitor argument proves a value Lipschitz bound uniform in \(\lambda\), rather than using the score's larger constant \(1/\lambda\).





The translated competitor makes the quadratic penalties identical, so the only difference comes from the original potential values. The original value Lipschitz constant therefore passes to the envelope unchanged. This argument is independent of the proximal scale and does not require a bound on how rapidly the envelope gradient varies. It is the magnitude of that gradient, rather than its Lipschitz constant, that is needed later.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Find a true minimum and bound the smooth force there}{Find a true minimum and bound the smooth force there}}


Strong convexity gives quadratic coercivity. Minimize \(F\) on a sufficiently large closed ball, with radius \(2\|\nabla F(0)\|/m+1\); the quadratic lower bound proves the resulting point minimizes globally. At a global minimum \(z\), the local-extremum derivative vanishes. Hence \(\nabla f(z)=-\nabla e(z)\) and \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\).






A quadratic lower bound on the total strongly convex potential makes it exceed a known competitor outside a finite ball. Compact minimization supplies an actual global mode. At this differentiable mode, \(\nabla(f+e)=0\), so the smooth score equals the negative envelope score. The uniform envelope-gradient magnitude therefore gives a finite anchor value \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\) for the smooth-score growth estimate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A bounded gradient remains bounded in L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{A bounded gradient remains bounded in L²}}


The norm of the derivative of a \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar function is at most \(K\); the gradient is its Riesz representative and has the same norm. For a pointwise bounded drift \(\|b(x)\|\le K\), almost everywhere representative equality gives \(\|b(Y)\|_{L^2}\le K\) on a probability space.






For a differentiable \(K\)-Lipschitz function, difference quotients in every unit direction are bounded in absolute value by \(K\). Passing to the derivative and taking the supremum over directions gives the gradient norm bound. If that bound holds pointwise, integration over a probability space gives \(\mathbb E\|b(Y)\|^2\le K^2\). No particle dimension or inverse envelope scale is introduced.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate a sharp quadratic bound about the mode}{Integrate a sharp quadratic bound about the mode}}


Lipschitz continuity and the mode bound give \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le L\|x-z\|+K\), so \(\|\nabla f(x)\|^2\le2L^2\|x-z\|^2+2K^2\). Sharp Gibbs concentration supplies \(\mathbb E_\pi\|x-z\|^2\le d/m\), yielding

\[\mathbb E_\pi\|\nabla f\|^2\le2L^2d/m+2K^2.\]

For any \(Y\) with actual Gibbs law \(\pi\), the pushforward integral formula transfers this estimate and taking square roots gives \(\|\nabla f(Y)\|_2\le\sqrt{2L^2d/m+2K^2}\).






Center the smooth score at a mode of the total potential, not necessarily a mode of the smooth part alone. Lipschitzness gives \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le L\|x-z\|+K\). Squaring and using \((a+b)^2\le2a^2+2b^2\) produces an integrable quadratic majorant. Sharp Gibbs concentration then gives the moment \(2L^2d/m+2K^2\), valid uniformly over any smoothing scales satisfying the same magnitude bound.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Lipschitz energy and the averaged lifted potential}{Lipschitz energy and the averaged lifted potential}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCoupling-lean}


The stochastic splitting proof needs an energy estimate for the actual averaged proximity map and a value Lipschitz estimate for the averaged particle potential. Both follow from genuine single-pair proximity, Jensen's inequality and the scaled replication identity. The resulting constants do not require a false identification with a shared-state proximal minimizer.




Let \(g\) be convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz, \(\lambda>0\), and \(N>0\). The following arguments prove energy estimates for the true pairwise proximal map and a value-Lipschitz bound for \(G=N^{-1}\sum_i g(J_i z)\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Obtain energy from value Lipschitzness}{Obtain energy from value Lipschitzness}}


At \(p=P_\lambda(x)\), the residual support at a comparator \(a\) controls \(f(p)-f(a)\). Bound \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\), expand \(\|x-a\|^2\), and use \((\|x-p\|-\lambda K)^2\ge0\). Thus \(\|p-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\lambda(f(x)-f(a))+\lambda^2K^2\). Sum over pairs, use Jensen for the shared parameter, and divide by \(N\) to get the exact averaged energy inequality with remainder \(\lambda^2K^2\).




The pointwise input is a genuine proximal minimizer \(p\). Its residual \(r=(x-p)/\lambda\) is a subgradient at \(p\) and has norm at most \(K\) when the potential is \(K\)-Lipschitz. Combining the support at \(p\), \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\), and the quadratic expansion yields \[\|p-y\|^2\le\|x-y\|^2-2\lambda(f(x)-f(y))+\lambda^2K^2.\] Sum this for the original pairs and use parameter averaging to pass to the scaled state. The input is value Lipschitzness; no bounded subgradient selection or unproved lifted-proximal identity is hidden in the estimate.





The residual support at the proximal output controls the comparator energy. To replace the output energy by the input energy, use the global value bound \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\). The remaining displacement expression is bounded by \(\lambda^2K^2\) through completion of a square. Summing these valid single-pair inequalities gives the state energy estimate with exactly the averaged potential difference.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Averaged pair distances control the physical-\allowbreak{}time potential}{Averaged pair distances control the physical-time potential}}


Define \(G(z)=F_g(z)/N\). Jensen and \(\sum_i\|J_i v\|^2=N\|v\|^2\) imply \(N^{-1}\sum_i\|J_i v\|\le\|v\|\). Therefore

\[|G(z)-G(w)|\le\frac KN\sum_i\|J_i(z-w)\|\le K\|z-w\|.\]

The same constant survives the averaging because the latent coordinates were scaled by \(N^{-1/2}\).






Cauchy–Schwarz for the finite particle average bounds the mean pair distance by the square root of the mean pair squared distance. The latter is exactly the scaled state distance by replication. Therefore averaging cancels the apparent particle factor in the sum of value differences. The physical-time potential \(G=F_g/N\) retains Lipschitz constant \(K\), which is used in the finite expected-energy estimates.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Shared-\allowbreak{}noise contraction of the actual PIPGLA chain}{Shared-noise contraction of the actual PIPGLA chain}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingContraction-lean}


For the actual splitting map, parameter averaging preserves nonexpansiveness of each joint proximity map. A shared innovation cancels before proximity, so the strongly convex smooth forward contraction survives the composition. This proves pathwise stability for every supplied innovation sequence and every positive proximal scale.




Let \(N>0\), let \(g_1\) be differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and let \(g_2\) be convex and bounded below. Assume \(0<m\le L_1\), \(\lambda>0\), and \(0\le\gamma\le2/(m+L_1)\). These are deterministic comparisons of two chains with the same innovations.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Jensen handles two independently averaged outputs}{Jensen handles two independently averaged outputs}}


Apply the scalar average-square inequality to the difference of the parameter outputs. Each latent difference has its exact scaled square. Thus

\[\|\operatorname{Pair}_P(z)-\operatorname{Pair}_P(w)\|^2\le N^{-1}\sum_i\|P(J_i z)-P(J_i w)\|^2.\]

If \(P\) is nonexpansive, each summand is bounded by \(\|J_i(z-w)\|^2\). Their sum is \(N\|z-w\|^2\), proving that the actual pairwise map is also nonexpansive.




Put \(A_i z=(\theta,\sqrt N z_i)\) and \(\Delta_i=P(A_i z)-P(A_i w)\). The shared parameter is an average, so Jensen gives \(N\|\overline\Delta_\theta\|^2\le\sum_i\|\Delta_{i,\theta}\|^2\); each latent coordinate is rescaled by \(N^{-1/2}\), so its corresponding squared identity is exact. Summing yields \[\|\operatorname{Pair}P(z)-\operatorname{Pair}P(w)\|^2\le\frac1N\sum_i\|\Delta_i\|^2.\] Nonexpansiveness of \(P\) and \(\sum_i\|A_i(z-w)\|^2=N\|z-w\|^2\) prove the claimed state-space bound. The positivity of \(N\) justifies both division and the unscaling.





The two averaged parameter outputs need not coincide pair by pair. Jensen handles their average difference, while latent squares have exact scaled identities. Applying pairwise nonexpansiveness and the replication identity gives a unit Lipschitz constant for the entire averaged map. No assertion that this map is a shared-state proximal minimizer is used in the argument.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A shared innovation cancels before proximity}{A shared innovation cancels before proximity}}


The lifted smooth gradient step has strong convexity \(Nm\), gradient constant \(NL_1\), and step \(\gamma/N\), so its contraction factor is \(1-m\gamma\ge0\). The same Gaussian or deterministic innovation added to both inputs cancels in their difference. Composition with the actual nonexpansive averaged proximal map gives

\[\|T_\lambda(z,\xi)-T_\lambda(w,\xi)\|\le(1-m\gamma)\|z-w\|.\]

Induction on the displayed recursion yields factor \((1-m\gamma)^n\). This proves stability; a separate stationary reference and local-consistency estimate are needed for convergence to a Gibbs target.




In one shared-noise comparison, the additive Gaussian vector cancels before applying the pairwise proximal map. The smooth forward step contracts by \(q=1-m\gamma\); the actual proximal minimizer is nonexpansive, so composing the two leaves the same factor. The step restriction proves \(q\ge0\), which is needed to multiply the induction inequality. Starting with \(e_0=\|z-w\|\), the successor case gives \(e_{k+1}\le q e_k\le q^{k+1}e_0\). This establishes pathwise stability under arbitrary supplied innovations, without stationarity or independence assumptions.





For the same innovation, the pre-proximal state difference is exactly the deterministic smooth forward difference. Its contraction factor is \(q=1-m\gamma\), which is nonnegative under the stated restriction. Composing with a nonexpansive map gives \(e_{k+1}\le q e_k\). Induction yields \(q^n e_0\), independently of Gaussianity or independence, because only equality of the innovations is needed.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Quadratic Hilbert identities and integrated convex energy}{Quadratic Hilbert identities and integrated convex energy}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingL2Energy-lean}


The deterministic energy estimate must be integrated with every term justified as a finite expectation. Lipschitz value growth and square-integrable states supply that integrability. Weighted quadratic inequalities preserve a useful contraction in squared error, while integrated convex supports put the nonsmooth reference force in the form needed for energy cancellation.




The pointwise proximal estimate is lifted to actual \(L^2\) random variables. All scalar energy expectations are proved integrable before distributing integrals or comparing them.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Weighted Young inequality preserves a linear squared contraction}{Weighted Young inequality preserves a linear squared contraction}}


For \(t>0\), expansion and \((t\|v\|-\|r\|)^2\ge0\) prove

\[\|v+r\|^2\le(1+t)\|v\|^2+(1+t^{-1})\|r\|^2.\]

If \(0<t\le1\) and \(\|v\|\le(1-t)\|e\|\), use \((1+t)(1-t)^2\le1-t\). The separate exact identity \(\|v+r+b\|^2=\|v+r\|^2+2\langle v+r+b,b\rangle-\|b\|^2\) keeps an integrated nonsmooth force inside a useful cross term.






Weighted Young uses the free positive parameter to control a mixed term without doubling the leading error. Choosing that parameter as \(mh\) later makes \((1+mh)(1-mh)^2\le1-mh\) when \(mh\le1\). The remaining defect term is multiplied by \(1+(mh)^{-1}\). Keeping the integrated nonsmooth force in a separate inner product is essential because its energy contribution will cancel rather than be bounded by a large norm.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Justify potential and quadratic expectations}{Justify potential and quadratic expectations}}


For a globally \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar \(f\), center at \(f(0)\); its growth is at most \(K\|X\|\). Hence \(f(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\) on a probability space. The actual Hilbert-space \(L^2\) inner product gives \(\|X\|_2^2=\int\|X(\omega)\|^2dP\), without any arbitrary representative-dependent value.




For a value-Lipschitz observable \(f\), \(|f(X)|\le K\|X\|+|f(0)|\); hence an \(L^2\) state on a probability space gives \(f(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\). The first proof implements this by centering \(f\) at zero. The second identity uses the definition of the \(L^2\) inner product and \(\langle X,X\rangle=\|X\|^2\): \(\|X\|_{L^2}^2=\int\|X(\omega)\|^2dP\). These facts ensure that the energy expressions below are ordinary finite Bochner integrals.





A Lipschitz scalar observable obeys \(|f(x)|\le|f(0)|+K\|x\|\). Its composition with an \(L^2\) state is therefore in \(L^2\), and on a probability space it is also integrable. Quadratic differences of two \(L^2\) states are integrable by the norm triangle inequality. Thus both energy expectations and squared distances in the subsequent inequality have ordinary finite values.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate the actual deterministic energy premise}{Integrate the actual deterministic energy premise}}


Suppose \(\|T(x)-y\|^2\le\|x-y\|^2-2h(f(x)-f(y))+C\), with \(T,f\) Lipschitz. Apply it pointwise to \(S,Y\in L^2\). The composition representative agrees almost everywhere with \(T(S)\), and every term is integrable. Monotonicity and linearity of the integral yield

\[\|T(S)-Y\|_2^2\le\|S-Y\|_2^2-2h(\mathbb E f(S)-\mathbb E f(Y))+C.\]




Apply the pointwise inequality to representatives of the two random states on their common full-measure event. The Lipschitz composition represents the proximal output almost surely. With finite integrability already established, integral monotonicity and linearity give the same energy inequality in \(L^2\). The additive constant is unchanged because the source has mass one, and no unproved exchange of infinite terms is involved.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate convex supports and split endpoint differences}{Integrate convex supports and split endpoint differences}}


Convex differentiability gives \(\langle S-Y,\nabla e(Y)\rangle\le e(S)-e(Y)\) pointwise. Integrate to obtain the same inequality for the \(L^2\) inner product. For a third variable \(Z\), write \(S-Z=(S-Y)+(Y-Z)\). This splits the cross term into the convex energy difference plus \(\langle Y-Z,\nabla e(Y)\rangle_{L^2}\), whose future-noise part can later be canceled.






For differentiable convex \(e\), the pointwise support inequality is \(\langle S-Y,\nabla e(Y)\rangle\le e(S)-e(Y)\). The gradient composition and state difference lie in \(L^2\), making their pairing integrable by Cauchy–Schwarz. Integrating therefore yields the corresponding Hilbert-space inner-product inequality. This is the bridge that later replaces a reference-force cross term by expected potential differences.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Local smooth drift defect and the future cross term}{Local smooth drift defect and the future cross term}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProcess-lean}


The reference integral equation controls increments by a drift part of order time and a Brownian part of order square root of time. Only the smooth component's Lipschitz constant is used to bound its local defect. For the nonsmooth cross term, future-noise orthogonality is used before a norm bound, retaining a smaller time-order remainder.




Let a continuous Hilbert-valued reference satisfy \(Y_t-Y_0=-\int_0^t g(Y_s)ds+B_t\), with \(\|g(Y_t)\|\le G\) and \(\|B_t\|\le\sigma\sqrt t\). Only the smooth-part Lipschitz constant enters the local defect.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate the smooth drift increment}{Integrate the smooth drift increment}}


The integral equation implies \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le Gt+\sigma\sqrt t\). If \(a\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz and \(0\le t\le h\), then \(\|a(Y_t)-a(Y_0)\|\le L(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)\). Integrating over an interval of length \(h\) yields

\[\left\|\int_0^h(a(Y_t)-a(Y_0))dt\right\|\le Lh(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h).\]


For the integral solution \(Y_t-Y_0=-\int_0^t g(Y_s)ds+B_t\), assume \(\|g(Y_s)\|\le G\) and \(\|B_t\|\le\nu\sqrt t\) in the Hilbert space under consideration. Then \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le tG+\nu\sqrt t\). If only the smooth part \(a\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, \[\left\|\int_0^h(a(Y_t)-a(Y_0))dt\right\|\le Lh(Gh+\nu\sqrt h).\] The code first bounds the integrand uniformly for \(0\le t\le h\), using monotonicity of \(t\) and \(\sqrt t\), and then applies the interval-integral norm inequality. The Lipschitz constant of the full drift ensures regularity but does not enter this numerical bound.





Taking the norm of the integral equation gives \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le Gt+\sigma\sqrt t\). Smooth-force Lipschitzness multiplies this increment by \(L\). For \(t\in[0,h]\), replace \(t\) and \(\sqrt t\) by their endpoint upper bounds, then multiply by the interval length. This proves the local defect estimate without using the full smoothed score's inverse-scale Lipschitz constant.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Cancel future noise before estimating the remainder}{Cancel future noise before estimating the remainder}}


Subtract the reference equations at \(t,h\) and use adjacent-interval additivity:

\[Y_t-Y_h=\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds-(B_h-B_t).\]

If \(\|b\|\le K\) and \(\langle b,B_h-B_t\rangle=0\), the noise term cancels exactly. Cauchy–Schwarz and the drift bound give \(\langle Y_t-Y_h,b\rangle\le(h-t)GK\). This avoids an order-\(\sqrt{h-t}\) loss.




Subtract the two integral equations and split the integral at \(t\): \[Y_t-Y_h=\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds-(B_h-B_t).\] Pair this identity with \(b\). The explicit orthogonality assumption cancels the future-noise contribution before any norm estimate, leaving \[\langle Y_t-Y_h,b\rangle\le\left\|\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds\right\|\|b\|\le(h-t)GK.\] In the probabilistic application this is an inner product in \(L^2\), so orthogonality means the expected inner product is zero; it is not a samplewise cancellation of Brownian increments.





Subtracting the equations at \(t\) and \(h\) expresses their difference using only the future drift integral and future noise increment. Pair with the past-measurable bounded force before applying Cauchy–Schwarz. Orthogonality removes the noise term exactly. The drift integral has norm at most \((h-t)G\), giving the remainder \((h-t)GK\), of first order in the remaining time.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Regroup genuinely independent source blocks}{Regroup genuinely independent source blocks}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-IndependenceHelpers-lean}


The Brownian splitting argument groups previously independent source blocks into the initialized past and the future increment. These measure-theoretic lemmas justify that regrouping at the level of joint laws. They retain the full joint-independence hypotheses, which are stronger than unrelated pairwise independence statements.




These measure-theoretic lemmas reorganize supplied joint independence. They do not infer joint independence from pairwise independence.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Two independent variables form a Boolean family}{Two independent variables form a Boolean family}}


Given \(X\perp Y\), define \(Z_{\rm false}=X\), \(Z_{\rm true}=Y\). The finite-family intersection criterion has four possible index subsets: the empty set, either singleton, or both indices. Probability mass one handles the empty case, the singletons are identities, and the two-element case is exactly \(P(X\in A,Y\in B)=P(X\in A)P(Y\in B)\).






For two variables, independence is the factorization of probabilities of simultaneous membership in measurable sets. Encoding them with a two-element index family adds no further condition, because every finite subfamily contains zero, one or two variables. The empty subfamily uses source mass one, the singleton cases are identities, and the two-variable case is exactly the supplied factorization.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Regroup a jointly independent triple}{Regroup a jointly independent triple}}


Assume \(X\perp(Y,Z)\) and \(Y\perp Z\), with measurable variables. The joint pushforward is \(\mu_X\otimes(\mu_Y\otimes\mu_Z)\). Use the measurable product-associativity equivalence to rewrite it as \((\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y)\otimes\mu_Z\). Projection also proves \(\mu_{(X,Y)}=\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y\), so \((X,Y)\perp Z\). The joint independence premise is essential.




The hypotheses say that \(X\) is independent of the whole pair \((Y,Z)\), and that \(Y\) and \(Z\) are independent. Therefore the triple law is \(\mu_X\otimes(\mu_Y\otimes\mu_Z)\). Product associativity rewrites it as \((\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y)\otimes\mu_Z\), proving independence of \((X,Y)\) and \(Z\). The proof uses equality of pushforward measures under the measurable associator, so this is joint independence; pairwise independence of three unrelated variables would not suffice.





Write the law of the triple explicitly and use the supplied independence to factor it into three marginal laws. Reassociating the product measure groups the first two marginals together. Their product is the joint law of \((X,Y)\), so the regrouped factorization proves \((X,Y)\perp Z\). The proof uses equality of measurable pushforwards, ensuring the conclusion remains valid for path-valued source blocks.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Solutions depend measurably on their initial state and past noise}{Solutions depend measurably on their initial state and past noise}}
\label{proof-module-UnadjustedLangevin-WeakForwardAdapted-lean}


The integral solution remains in any complete invariant subspace containing the initial state and noise. Applying this principle to the subspace of random variables measurable with respect to a smaller sigma algebra proves the needed adaptedness statement.



The variables measurable from initialization and noise up to time \(T\) form a closed subspace of \(L^2\). Composition with the Lipschitz drift preserves that subspace, as do finite sums and limits of Bochner integrals. Solving the integral equation inside the subspace and applying uniqueness shows that the ambient solution remains there. This derives dependence on past data from the construction; it does not impose measurability of future noise on earlier solution values.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Preservation of an invariant closed subspace}{Preservation of an invariant closed subspace}}


Solve the same integral equation inside the complete submodule \(S\), using drift invariance, \(x\in S\), and \(B(t)\in S\). Include this solution back into the ambient space; continuous linearity of the inclusion commutes with the Bochner integral. Ambient uniqueness identifies it with \(Y\), so every \(Y(t)\) lies in \(S\).



The closed subspace is complete and invariant under the drift. Solving inside it and using uniqueness of the ambient integral equation prevents the solution from acquiring components outside the subspace.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurability of the lifted drift}{Measurability of the lifted drift}}


A Lipschitz map is continuous, so composing it with an almost-everywhere strongly measurable \(X\) preserves that measurability for the specified sub-sigma-algebra. The representative of the lifted drift agrees almost everywhere with this composition. Transport across that equality proves the result independently of the representative selected for the \(L^2\) class.



Composition preserves measurability for the chosen smaller sigma-algebra because the drift is continuous. Almost-everywhere representative equality transfers this property to the lifted class independently of the representative chosen for it.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Past measurability of the integral solution}{Past measurability of the integral solution}}


The \(L^2\) variables measurable for a sub-sigma-algebra form a complete closed submodule. The preceding composition theorem makes that submodule invariant under the lifted drift. Apply submodule preservation using measurability of the initialization and every noise value up to \(T\); the integral solution then belongs to the same submodule at each time.



The past-measurable classes form a closed subspace of \(L^2\). Initialization and the relevant noise values belong to it, so the invariant-subspace principle applies to the integral solution up to the chosen horizon.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The sigma-\allowbreak{}algebra generated by past observations}{The sigma-algebra generated by past observations}}


Define the past sigma-algebra as the smallest sigma-algebra making the initialization and every noise observation \(B(t)\), \(0\le t\le T\), measurable. It is the join of their generated sigma-algebras, so it contains exactly the information needed for the stated past-measurability conclusion.



The join is the smallest sigma-algebra making the initialization and all noise observations up to time \(T\) measurable. Thus it expresses exactly the information allowed in the adaptedness conclusion.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Inclusion in the ambient sigma-\allowbreak{}algebra}{Inclusion in the ambient sigma-algebra}}


Every generator of the past is measurable in the ambient sigma-algebra. The universal property of a supremum of sigma-algebras therefore gives \(\mathcal F_T\le\mathcal F\). This inclusion is required to use the closed \(L^2\) subspace of variables measurable from the past.



Each generating sigma-algebra is contained in the ambient one because the original observations are measurable. Taking their join preserves that inclusion, validating the smaller-space \(L^2\) construction.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurability of the initial state from the past}{Measurability of the initial state from the past}}


The sigma-algebra generated by the initial variable is one summand of the defining join for \(\mathcal F_T\). Its inclusion makes the initial variable measurable for \(\mathcal F_T\), and the measurable representative of an \(L^2\) variable supplies the almost-everywhere strong-measurability formulation used by the submodule theorem.



Initialization is included as an explicit generator of the past. This retains any dependence present in the initial random state while excluding information from future innovations.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurability of every previous noise value}{Measurability of every previous noise value}}


For \(t\in[0,T]\), the sigma-algebra generated by \(B(t)\) is included in the indexed join defining the past. Measurability of the representative with respect to its generated sigma-algebra then extends along this inclusion, giving \(B(t)\)'s measurability from \(\mathcal F_T\).



For \(t\le T\), the observation \(B(t)\) is one of the generators. Its measurability follows by the inclusion of that generated sigma-algebra in the joined past.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Adaptedness to the natural past}{Adaptedness to the natural past}}


Apply the sub-sigma-algebra solution theorem to the explicitly generated \(\mathcal F_T\). The three preceding lemmas discharge its ambient inclusion, initialization, and noise-measurability premises. Thus every solution value up to \(T\) is measurable from initialization and past noise, with no adaptedness assumption added to the solution.



Applying preservation with the constructed past shows that the solution uses only initialization and previous noise values. This is derived from the integral equation and uniqueness, rather than assumed as a separate solution property.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Derive Brownian future orthogonality from the actual past}{Derive Brownian future orthogonality from the actual past}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean}


The key cross term vanishes because the reference observable is measurable from the actual initialized Brownian past, whereas the future increment is independent and centered. The argument constructs this past/\allowbreak{}future independence from full scalar-path independence and the adapted integral solution. The orthogonality is in expected inner product, rather than a samplewise identity.




Let the scalar Brownian coordinates have independent full paths, measurable evaluations, and initialization independent of the whole Brownian family. The actual Langevin integral solution is measurable from initialization and the stopped noise past.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Assemble independent vector past and shifted future}{Assemble independent vector past and shifted future}}


Each scalar Brownian path has independent shifted future \(B_i(T+s)-B_i(T)\) and stopped past \(B_i(\min(t,T))\). Encode these as a Boolean pair for each coordinate. Full-path independence makes the coordinate rows independent; flatten the rows and Boolean columns, then take the disjoint sets of past and future indices. Regrouping proves independence of the entire vector path families.




The stopped past records each coordinate path up to time \(t\), while the future block records increments after \(t\). Brownian independent increments give this separation within each scalar coordinate. Independence of the whole coordinate paths permits regrouping the coordinatewise pairs into one vector past and one vector future. This is a statement about the generated product sigma-algebras, so it remains valid when the future vector is paired with any measurable function of the entire past.





Within each scalar Brownian path, independent increments separate the stopped past from all shifted future increments. Independence of complete coordinate paths lets these coordinatewise pairs be assembled into one jointly independent family. Disjoint selection then groups all past coordinates on one side and all future coordinates on the other. This yields independence of entire vector-valued path blocks, enough for every measurable past observable.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Relate concrete noise to its L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} representative}{Relate concrete noise to its L² representative}}


Define the pointwise vector \(\mathcal B_t=\sqrt2(B(t_+)-B(0))\), using the nonnegative-time conversion \(t_+=\max(t,0)\). The \(L^2\) Brownian-noise construction agrees almost everywhere with this formula. Each coordinate evaluation is measurable, so finite vector assembly, subtraction and scaling prove measurability of the raw representative.





The raw noise at a fixed time is a finite vector of measurable scalar evaluations, differences and a fixed scale factor. Its coordinate second moments make it square integrable. The constructed \(L^2\) noise and this pointwise vector agree almost surely, so computations of means, laws and independence can be transferred between them. The nonnegative time convention only specifies the domain of Brownian evaluation.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Add the independent initialization to the stopped past}{Add the independent initialization to the stopped past}}


The stopped noise path and a future increment are independent. Initialization is independent of their joint pair, so the regrouping lemma proves that initialization together with the stopped past is independent of the future. An auxiliary result then compares sigma-algebras: every variable almost everywhere strongly measurable for the natural initialization/\allowbreak{}noise past inherits this independence. Almost everywhere replacement transfers the result to actual \(L^2\) representatives.




The initialized natural past contains both the initial state and the stopped Brownian path. Independence of initialization from the entire Brownian source, together with past/\allowbreak{}future Brownian independence, permits the triple regrouping \((\text{initial},\text{past})\mid\text{future}\). An almost-strongly-measurable past variable can be replaced by a measurable representative without changing its law or independence. This is the role of the representative arguments: independence is transferred through almost-everywhere equality, rather than assumed for the later integral solution.





Initialization is independent of the complete noise source, including both stopped past and future. Combining this fact with Brownian past/\allowbreak{}future independence gives independence of the pair consisting of initialization and past from the future increment. A variable measurable from that initialized past inherits the same independence by composition. Almost-sure replacement preserves both the law and this factorization, allowing actual square-integrable representatives to be used.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Centering and independence imply Hilbert orthogonality}{Centering and independence imply Hilbert orthogonality}}


Every scalar Brownian coordinate has mean zero. Commute the finite coordinate projections through the Bochner integral to get \(\mathbb E B_t=0\), then subtract and scale for \(\mathbb E\mathcal B_t=0\). For independent \(L^2\) variables \(X,W\), bilinear integration gives \(\langle X,W\rangle_{L^2}=\langle\mathbb E X,\mathbb E W\rangle\). If \(W\) is centered this is zero.





For independent square-integrable vectors, their inner product is integrable by Cauchy–Schwarz. Independence and bilinearity factor its expectation as the inner product of their means. A Brownian increment has mean zero coordinatewise, and finite vector assembly preserves that zero mean. Therefore its expected pairing with any independent square-integrable past vector vanishes exactly, without estimating the size of either vector.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Apply adaptedness of the constructed integral solution}{Apply adaptedness of the constructed integral solution}}


The invariant-subspace construction for the actual integral equation proves that \(Y_t\) is measurable for the natural past. A Lipschitz observable \(b(Y_t)\) remains past measurable and in \(L^2\). The preceding independence and zero-mean results therefore prove

\[\langle b(Y_t),\mathcal B_h-\mathcal B_t\rangle_{L^2}=0,\qquad0\le t\le h.\]

This essential cancellation is a conclusion of the Brownian and integral-equation construction.




The constructed solution is adapted to the initialized natural past, so the Lipschitz observable \(b(Y_t)\) is measurable there. The future Brownian increment is independent of this past and has zero mean. Both variables belong to \(L^2\), hence their inner product is integrable, and independence factors its expectation as \[\mathbb E\langle b(Y_t),B_h-B_t\rangle=\left\langle\mathbb E b(Y_t),\mathbb E(B_h-B_t)\right\rangle=0.\] The \(L^2\) inner product is precisely this integral. Thus the final orthogonality input of the splitting proof follows from adaptedness, centering and the constructed Brownian law.





The integral-equation construction ensures the reference state at time \(t\) depends only on initialization and noise up to \(t\). Composing with a Lipschitz drift observable preserves that measurability and square integrability. Apply initialized-past independence to the future increment over \([t,h]\), then use its zero mean. The resulting cancellation removes the otherwise order-\(\sqrt{h-t}\) contribution from the splitting cross-energy estimate.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Cancel the original nonsmooth energy in expectation}{Cancel the original nonsmooth energy in expectation}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCrossEnergy-lean}


Convex support turns the nonsmooth reference-force pairing into expected envelope energies. Stationarity aligns its time-dependent reference energy with the endpoint energy, and a finite value gap compares the envelope with the original nonsmooth potential. This cancels the original energy appearing in the actual proximal estimate, leaving controlled gap and drift remainders.




Let \(e\) be convex and differentiable, let \(e\le f\le e+\delta\), and let \(Y_t\) be a stationary reference for the smoothed energy with the actual integral equation and future-noise orthogonality. Lipschitz value bounds ensure all expectations exist.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrate a one-\allowbreak{}sided uniform value gap}{Integrate a one-sided uniform value gap}}


If \(f(x)\le e(x)+\delta\) pointwise and both functions are Lipschitz, their compositions with any \(L^2\) variable \(X\) are integrable. Integral monotonicity and total mass one give \(\mathbb E f(X)-\mathbb E e(X)\le\delta\). This is the precise one-sided expectation lemma used in the cross-energy argument.




If \(f\le e+\delta\), the two Lipschitz observables of an \(L^2\) state are integrable. Thus \(\mathbb Ef(X)\le\mathbb Ee(X)+\delta P(\Omega)=\mathbb Ee(X)+\delta\). The proof checks this integrability before splitting the integral. This comparison will be used once at the algorithm's intermediate state with \(e\le f\), and once at the reference endpoint with \(f\le e+\delta\).





Because the two observables are Lipschitz, composition with a square-integrable state gives integrable potential values. Integral monotonicity may therefore be combined with ordinary linearity. The constant gap integrates to \(\delta\), since the source probability has mass one. This elementary bound is applied to actual finite expected energies, with no differentiation of the original nonsmooth potential.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine convex support, stationarity and the future estimate}{Combine convex support, stationarity and the future estimate}}


The integrated convex support bounds \(\langle S-Y_h,\nabla e(Y_t)\rangle\) by \(\mathbb E e(S)-\mathbb E e(Y_t)+\langle Y_t-Y_h,\nabla e(Y_t)\rangle\). Stationarity replaces \(\mathbb E e(Y_t)\) by \(\mathbb E e(Y_h)\). Orthogonality and bounded drift/\allowbreak{}score give the remaining term at most \(hGK\). Commute the fixed inner-product functional through the time integral. Finally the gap \(e\le f\le e+\delta\) changes the two expected energies by at most \(\delta\), proving

\[2\left\langle S-Y_h,\int_0^h\nabla e(Y_t)dt\right\rangle-2h(\mathbb E f(S)-\mathbb E f(Y_h))\le2h\delta+2h^2GK.\]


For \(b_t=\nabla e(Y_t)\) in \(L^2\), convex support and the future-increment estimate imply \[\langle S-Y_h,b_t\rangle\le\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_t)+(h-t)GK.\] Stationarity replaces \(\mathbb Ee(Y_t)\) by \(\mathbb Ee(Y_h)\). Integrate on \([0,h]\); continuity of \(b_t\) justifies moving the bounded linear inner-product functional through the Bochner integral. This yields an upper bound \(h[\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_h)]+h^2GK\).





Split \(S-Y_h=(S-Y_t)+(Y_t-Y_h)\). Convex support controls the first pairing, and future orthogonality plus drift bounds control the second. Stationarity replaces \(\mathbb E e(Y_t)\) by \(\mathbb E e(Y_h)\). Integrating over time gives an envelope-energy difference. At \(S\) use \(e\le f\), and at \(Y_h\) use \(f\le e+\delta\), leaving only one \(\delta\) in the difference.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Derive the synchronous one-\allowbreak{}step splitting bound}{Derive the synchronous one-step splitting bound}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOneStep-lean}


The synchronous one-step estimate is derived by decomposing the error into a contracted smooth forward difference, a local smooth defect and an integrated envelope force. The actual proximal energy inequality and the stationary cross-energy comparison cancel the nonsmooth energy. Weighted Young then leaves a linear squared-error contraction with an explicit finite forcing.




In a Hilbert \(L^2\) space, let \(f_1\) be \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, and let the actual Lipschitz map \(T\) satisfy the nonsmooth energy inequality for \(f\). The reference solves the actual stationary smoothed integral equation for \(f_1+e\), where \(e\le f\le e+\delta\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Keep every local forcing term explicit}{Keep every local forcing term explicit}}


For \(m,h>0\), drift moment \(G\), noise moment factor \(\sigma\), bounded nonsmooth score \(K\) and value gap \(\delta\), define

\[\mathcal F=(1+(mh)^{-1})[Lh(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)]^2+2h\delta+2h^2GK+h^2K^2.\]

The four terms are the weighted smooth local defect, the envelope value gap, the drift cross term, and the genuine proximal energy remainder.





The smooth local defect is squared only after multiplying by the weighted factor \(1+(mh)^{-1}\). The remaining three contributions come from the finite envelope gap, the reference drift crossed with the bounded envelope score, and the actual proximal remainder. Separating these four terms records their different sources and permits a later scalar estimate showing that their sum is second order in the step.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{An exact decomposition makes the energy cancel}{An exact decomposition makes the energy cancel}}


Let \(S=X-h\nabla f_1(X)+B_h\), \(v=(X-h\nabla f_1(X))-(Y_0-h\nabla f_1(Y_0))\), \(R=\int_0^h[\nabla f_1(Y_t)-\nabla f_1(Y_0)]dt\), and \(H=\int_0^h\nabla e(Y_t)dt\). The integral equation gives the exact identity \(S-Y_h=v+R+H\). Expand its square using the rearranged identity; integrate the actual proximal energy inequality for \(T(S)\). The cross-energy lemma cancels the resulting expected \(f\)-difference. Sharp smooth-step contraction and weighted Young inequality bound \(\|v+R\|^2\), while the local-defect lemma bounds \(R\). Discarding \(-\|H\|^2\le0\) yields

\[\|T(S)-Y_h\|_2^2\le(1-mh)\|X-Y_0\|_2^2+\mathcal F.\]

Here \(m\le L\) and \(h\le2/(m+L)\) imply \(mh\le1\). Every reference premise is later supplied by the canonical construction.




Set \(a=\nabla f_1\), \(b=\nabla e\), \(S=X-ha(X)+B_h\), \(v=[X-ha(X)]-[Y_0-ha(Y_0)]\), \(R=\int_0^h[a(Y_t)-a(Y_0)]dt\), and \(H=\int_0^h b(Y_t)dt\). Splitting the actual reference integral equation gives the exact identity \(S-Y_h=v+R+H\). The norm rearrangement is \[\|S-Y_h\|^2=\|v+R\|^2+2\langle S-Y_h,H\rangle-\|H\|^2.\]





The decomposition is exact because the same additive noise appears in the algorithm's forward step and the reference integral equation. After applying the proximal energy inequality, the integrated envelope-force pairing cancels its original potential difference up to the cross-energy remainder. Weighted Young preserves factor \(1-mh\) in the old squared error. Dropping the nonpositive integrated-force square leaves the stated forcing, without a separate stochastic recurrence assumption.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Take the infimum of squared finite coupling estimates}{Take the infimum of squared finite coupling estimates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTransport-lean}


A squared coupling estimate with common nonnegative coefficients can be passed to Wasserstein distance without selecting an optimal plan. Finite input distance supplies finite approximating couplings. The greatest-lower-bound argument below keeps the additive forcing outside the coupling infimum and justifies all real-valued squares.




Assume \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\), \(q,c\ge0\), and \(W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\le q\operatorname{cost}(p)^2+c\) for every finite-cost coupling \(p\) of \(\mu,\nu\). No optimal coupling is needed.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Use the greatest lower bound characterization}{Use the greatest lower bound characterization}}


If \(q=0\), one finite coupling already gives the result. If the target square is at most \(c\), the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise set \(r=\sqrt{(W_2(\mu',\nu')^2-c)/q}\). Each coupling estimate implies \(r\le\operatorname{cost}(p)\); infinite-cost couplings also satisfy this extended inequality. Hence \(r\le\inf_p\operatorname{cost}(p)=W_2(\mu,\nu)\). Square this nonnegative inequality and multiply by \(q\) to obtain

\[W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\le qW_2(\mu,\nu)^2+c.\]

Finiteness justifies every extended-to-real conversion.




Write \(A=W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\), and suppose every finite-cost input coupling satisfies \(A\le qC^2+c\). If \(q=0\), one finite-cost coupling already proves \(A\le c\). If \(A\le c\), the result is immediate from \(q\ge0\). Otherwise set \(r=\sqrt{(A-c)/q}\); then every finite coupling has cost at least \(r\), and infinite-cost couplings also satisfy this lower bound in the extended order.





Equivalently, choose input couplings with costs tending down to the finite infimum. Their squared costs tend to \(W_2(\mu,\nu)^2\) by continuity of squaring on nonnegative real numbers. The same output bound holds for every such coupling, so taking the limit gives the displayed inequality. The explicit case split handles zero coefficient and a nonpositive residual before dividing, avoiding hidden positivity assumptions.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Construct a stationary Brownian coupling for one true transition}{Construct a stationary Brownian coupling for one true transition}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCanonical-lean}


The analytic one-step premises are realized on the product of an arbitrary finite-cost initial coupling and a canonical Brownian source. The reference is the actual stationary Langevin solution for the smoothed Gibbs potential. Its law, moment bounds and future orthogonality are constructed, while the Brownian endpoint identifies the algorithm's exact independent-Gaussian transition law.




The generic splitting map is \(\mathcal T_h(x,\xi)=T(x-h\nabla f_1(x)+\sqrt{2h}\xi)\). The reference target is the actual Gibbs law of \(F=f_1+e\). Assumptions concern potentials, true energy, bounded gradients and a finite input coupling.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurable update and exact gradient additivity}{Measurable update and exact gradient additivity}}


The map is the composition of the measurable Gaussian forward update with the Lipschitz map \(T\). Differentiability proves \(\nabla(f_1+e)=\nabla f_1+\nabla e\). Almost everywhere representative identities then prove additivity of the corresponding \(L^2\) drift maps, as an equality of actual \(L^2\) elements.





The smooth forward map is continuous jointly in state and Gaussian input, and the energy map is Lipschitz. Their composition is therefore measurable and defines a transition probability. Differentiability of both potential components gives the sum of their gradients. Composing with square-integrable states respects this equality almost surely, so the reference drift is exactly the score of the sum potential.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Extend any finite-\allowbreak{}cost coupling by canonical Brownian noise}{Extend any finite-cost coupling by canonical Brownian noise}}


Start with any coupling \(c\) between \(\mu\) and the Gibbs target, with finite quadratic cost. Extend it by the canonical independent Brownian source. The initial reference has the exact Gibbs law and finite moments; the constructed Langevin integral solution stays stationary. Its natural-past measurability and Brownian independence prove the future orthogonality. Uniform moments give

\[G=\sqrt{2L^2d/m+2K^2}+K,\qquad\sigma=\sqrt{2d}.\]

Use the Brownian increment over \([0,h]\) as the Gaussian update noise; its independence gives the exact Markov marginal. Apply the derived analytic one-step theorem. The explicit output pair is a finite coupling and proves

\[W_2(\mu\mathcal T_h,\pi)^2\le(1-mh)\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+\mathcal F(m,h,L,G,\sigma,K,\delta).\]

The construction discharges stationarity, Gaussian laws, finite moments and orthogonality; they are not extra convergence hypotheses.




Start from an arbitrary finite-cost coupling \(c\) between the input law and \(\pi\propto e^{-(f_1+e)}\). Extend it by an independent canonical Brownian source, producing square-integrable initial states \(X,Z\) with the required marginals. The actual Lipschitz integral equation constructs \(Y_t\) from \(Z\), and the Gibbs stationarity theorem proves \(\operatorname{Law}(Y_t)=\pi\) throughout the interval.





Under the product source, the first initial marginal has the input law and the second has Gibbs law. Independent Brownian noise evolves the latter by the constructed Langevin equation, whose stationarity theorem preserves the Gibbs law. The normalized endpoint increment is fresh standard Gaussian noise for the former. Their updated pair is thus a finite-cost coupling of the actual transition and the target, to which the derived analytic bound applies.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Actual splitting laws, L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} realizations and iid law transfer}{Actual splitting laws, L² realizations and iid law transfer}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingLaw-lean}


The splitting recursion applies actual pairwise proximity after the smooth Gaussian forward step. Its global Lipschitz properties supply measurability and finite second moments. Equality of pointwise and square-integrable realizations fixes the actual output law, and independent innovation inputs identify it with a canonical product-source Markov law.




The pointwise splitting update applies the averaged true proximal map after a noisy smooth step. Convexity and a lower bound for \(g_2\), positive \(\lambda\), \(N>0\) and a Lipschitz smooth gradient make this update measurable and preserve finite quadratic moments.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Prove measurability of the exact pointwise update}{Prove measurability of the exact pointwise update}}


Nonexpansiveness proves \(\operatorname{Lip}(\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\lambda})\le1\). The forward map \((z,\xi)\mapsto z-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\xi\) is measurable by continuity of its terms; compose it with proximity. Induction proves equality of the custom pointwise chain with the generic noise-driven chain.





The forward map is jointly continuous in state and innovation because the smooth score is Lipschitz. The averaged proximal map is nonexpansive and hence continuous. Their composition is measurable. Applying the same composition repeatedly gives a measurable finite-time map of initialization and innovations, which is the common recursion used in the law-transfer argument.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Realize the actual chain inside L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{Realize the actual chain inside L²}}


Define the algorithm law as the pushforward by the explicit chain. Define an \(L^2\) update by applying the Lipschitz pairwise map to the \(L^2\) noisy Euler update. Their representatives agree almost everywhere by the composition and addition identities in \(L^2\). Inductively every iterate lies in \(L^2\) and agrees almost everywhere with its pointwise recursion. This constructs moment control without Gaussianity or equilibrium assumptions.






The forward map has affine linear growth, and so does the Lipschitz proximal map. Thus square-integrable inputs produce square-integrable outputs. At each step their representatives agree almost surely with the pointwise composed update. Intersecting the necessary full-measure events and inducting identifies the two chains at every finite time. This proves actual-law moments without relying on a stationary reference or a convergence inequality.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Probability mass of the actual output law}{Probability mass of the actual output law}}


Almost everywhere equal representatives have equal pushforward measures, so \(\rho_n=Q\circ X_n^{-1}\) for the constructed \(L^2\) iterate. Its almost everywhere measurability makes this a genuine probability law: the preimage of the whole state space has \(Q\)-mass one.




The law of a measurable random variable on a probability space has mass one because the preimage of the whole target is the whole source. Almost-sure replacement of that variable does not change its pushforward. Therefore the explicit splitting law inherits normalization from its square-integrable realization. This property is separate from the stronger second-moment conclusion and is available before transport is considered.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Transfer any iid realization to the canonical product law}{Transfer any iid realization to the canonical product law}}


Assume the initial variable is independent of the whole innovation path and the innovations form an independent family with common law \(\eta\). The joint input law is \(\mathcal L(X_0)\otimes\eta^{\otimes\mathbb N}\). Applying the measurable noise-driven recursion gives exactly the canonical chain law. The proof uses the actual independence and common-law hypotheses, without assuming a stochastic error estimate.






The initialized innovation-path law is \(\mu_0\otimes\eta^{\otimes\mathbb N}\).
