The time-\(n\) state is a measurable deterministic function of this input, so its distribution is determined by that product law. Two realizations with the same initial law and iid innovation law consequently have identical finite-time outputs. This allows a convenient auxiliary product source to be used for coupling while still analyzing the supplied algorithm.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Canonical Gaussian law of the actual splitting algorithm}{Canonical Gaussian law of the actual splitting algorithm}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingGaussianLaw-lean}


Independent standard scalar Gaussian coordinates determine a standard state-space Gaussian vector at each time and an iid vector path across time. The measurable splitting recursion depends only on this path law and its independent initial law. Hence the canonical scalar-product and vector-product descriptions yield the same actual PIPGLA distributions.




The scalar-coordinate Gaussian hypotheses determine the complete vector innovation law and its infinite iid path. Hence every realization with a fixed initial law gives the same actual PIPGLA output law.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Assemble scalar coordinates into iid standard Gaussian vectors}{Assemble scalar coordinates into iid standard Gaussian vectors}}


The coordinate laws and their joint independence imply \(\xi_k\sim\mathcal N(0,I)\) as a full state vector, and independence of these vectors across \(k\). Together with initialization/\allowbreak{}path independence, the generic iid transfer theorem gives the chain law driven by \(\mathcal N(0,I)^{\otimes\mathbb N}\).






Each finite vector has the product of its coordinate standard Gaussian laws. Independence of disjoint time-coordinate index sets then gives independence of the full vectors at distinct times. These two facts determine the vector path product law. Together with initialization independence they supply exactly the inputs of the general iid recursion theorem, without requiring any stationary law of the chain.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Use either canonical noise-\allowbreak{}path representation}{Use either canonical noise-path representation}}


The countable product of scalar Gaussian coordinates, grouped by iteration, has the same noise-path pushforward law as the vector product. Apply the measurable chain to this identity. Two probability-space realizations with the same initialization law therefore both equal the same canonical Gaussian chain law at time \(n\), proving equality of their actual output laws.






Grouping scalar coordinates into vectors is a measurable coordinate rearrangement. Its pushforward carries the scalar product Gaussian measure to the vector product Gaussian measure. Applying the same finite recursion to either source therefore gives identical laws. For two different probability spaces, only the common initial law remains to be matched; the concrete representatives and noise-source constructions disappear from the distributional conclusion.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Uniform Moreau gaps in scaled particle coordinates}{Uniform Moreau gaps in scaled particle coordinates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ParticleSmoothing-lean}


Single-pair Moreau estimates lift to the particle potential by exact finite sums. The full lifted envelope score has magnitude at most \(NK\), while its finite value gap is at most \(N\eta K^2/2\). These estimates use value Lipschitzness and remain distinct from the larger gradient Lipschitz constant involving \(1/\eta\).




Write \(F_g(z)=\sum_i g(J_i z)\), with \(N>0\). If \(g\) is convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz, its lifted envelope term has a bounded gradient independent of the positive Moreau scale.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Lift value Lipschitzness and a bounded envelope score}{Lift value Lipschitzness and a bounded envelope score}}


The averaged potential is \(K\)-Lipschitz, so multiplying it by \(N\) proves \(\operatorname{Lip}(F_g)\le NK\). The envelope has the same value Lipschitz constant \(K\), hence \(F_{g^\eta}\) has constant \(NK\) too. Its actual gradient norm is therefore at most \(NK\), uniformly over \(\eta>0\).




Multiplying the preceding averaged-potential inequality by \(N\) proves that the lifted sum has value Lipschitz constant \(NK\). The true Moreau envelope has the same value Lipschitz constant \(K\), so its lifted score satisfies \(\|\nabla V_{N,\eta}^{(2)}\|\le NK\). This follows from the norm of the derivative of a globally Lipschitz function, not from its score Lipschitz constant \(N/\eta\). Consequently this magnitude bound remains uniform as \(\eta\downarrow0\).





The averaged lifted potential has value Lipschitz constant \(K\), so the full sum has constant \(NK\). The envelope preserves the single-pair value constant, and differentiability converts that value bound into a gradient magnitude bound. This supplies \(\|\nabla F_{g_2^\eta}\|\le NK\) uniformly in \(\eta\). The force can vary rapidly at small scale, yet its magnitude stays bounded.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Sum the finite gap and preserve the split decomposition}{Sum the finite gap and preserve the split decomposition}}


Apply \(0\le g-g^\eta\le\eta K^2/2\) at every joint pair and add:

\[0\le F_g(z)-F_{g^\eta}(z)\le N\eta K^2/2.\]

Finite sum distributivity also proves \(F_{g_1+g_2^\eta}=F_{g_1}+F_{g_2^\eta}\) as an equality of functions. These exact identities supply the deterministic inputs to the stochastic splitting estimate.




At every pair, \(0\le g_2-g_2^\eta\le\eta K^2/2\). The finite sum has exactly \(N\) terms, giving \[0\le V_N^{(2)}-V_{N,\eta}^{(2)}\le N\eta K^2/2.\] The decomposition lemma is equality of functions: expand \(V_N(g_1+g_2^\eta)\) and distribute the finite sum to obtain \(V_N(g_1)+V_N(g_2^\eta)\). This identifies the two gradient pieces used by the reference process with the actual smoothed potential.





Every summand has a nonnegative gap bounded by the same \(\eta K^2/2\). Adding exactly \(N\) terms gives the lifted gap without any norm inequality. Distributing the lifted sum over the two potential components gives a function identity, so differentiation splits their scores under the already proved differentiability. These identities align the reference drift with the actual smoothed Gibbs potential.

\section{\texorpdfstring{A finite coefficient for the splitting error floor}{A finite coefficient for the splitting error floor}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingRate-lean}


The local forcing can be bounded by \(mh^2C\) when the envelope value gap is at most \(hK^2/2\). The explicit coefficient uses curvature, smooth-score regularity, drift and noise moments, and a uniform nonsmooth-score magnitude. It contains no inverse envelope gradient constant, enabling a vanishing small-step transport floor.




The derived forcing uses no inverse envelope gradient constant. This scalar estimate turns it into an \(O(h^2)\) one-step squared error when the value gap is \(\delta\le hK^2/2\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define and sign the explicit coefficient}{Define and sign the explicit coefficient}}


For \(m>0\), \(G,K\ge0\), define

\[C(m,L,G,\sigma,K)=\frac{4L^2}{m^2}\left(\frac{G^2}{m}+\sigma^2\right)+\frac2m(GK+K^2).\]

Squares and positive denominators prove \(C\ge0\). This constant may be conservative but remains finite under its stated scalar assumptions.





Every summand of \(C\) is nonnegative under \(m>0\) and nonnegative magnitude constants. The coefficient is finite because its only denominators are powers of the positive curvature. This separates numerical conservativeness from analytic validity: the constant need not be optimal to yield a finite-step convergence guarantee, but all its dimension and particle factors remain explicit.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Bound the exact local forcing}{Bound the exact local forcing}}


For \(h\le1/m\), use \((Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)^2\le2G^2h^2+2\sigma^2h\le2h(G^2/m+\sigma^2)\) and \(h+1/m\le2/m\). The weighted defect is then at most \(4L^2h^2(G^2/m+\sigma^2)/m\). The gap contributes \(2h\delta\le h^2K^2\). Adding the remaining \(2h^2GK+h^2K^2\) proves \(\mathcal F\le mh^2C\). Thus iteration can produce the squared floor \(hC\).




The local defect contains \((Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)^2\). First use \((u+v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2\) and \(h\le1/m\) to obtain \[(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)^2\le2h(G^2/m+\sigma^2).\] Next, \((1+(mh)^{-1})h^2\cdot2h=2h^2(h+1/m)\le4h^2/m\). The finite value gap contributes \(2h\delta\le h^2K^2\); together with the pre-existing \(h^2K^2\) and \(2h^2GK\), these terms give exactly \(mh^2\,C\). Every denominator is protected by \(m,h>0\), and the coefficient has no inverse smoothing parameter.





First bound the drift-plus-noise square using \((a+b)^2\le2a^2+2b^2\). Since \(h\le1/m\), every extra power of \(h\) is controlled by curvature. The weighted local defect is then at most a fixed coefficient times \(h^2\). The finite gap contributes another \(h^2K^2\). Adding the remaining drift-score and proximal terms gives exactly \(mh^2C\), whose geometric denominator later cancels \(mh\).

\section{\texorpdfstring{Actual equal-\allowbreak{}step PIPGLA transport convergence}{Actual equal-step PIPGLA transport convergence}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingEndpoint-lean}


The constructed one-step coupling becomes a recurrence for actual Gaussian Markov laws after taking initial-coupling infima. Its forcing has a finite coefficient independent of the equal smoothing and forward step. Iteration yields a squared transport floor linear in that step, and exact particle scaling identifies the corresponding actual equal-step PIPGLA bound.


\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Derive the one-\allowbreak{}step transport recurrence}{Derive the one-step transport recurrence}}


The admissibility condition and \(m\le L\) give \(mh\le1\). Canonical stationary coupling proves a finite output cost for every finite input coupling. Taking their infimum yields both finiteness and

\[W_2(\rho_{k+1},\pi)^2\le(1-mh)W_2(\rho_k,\pi)^2+\mathcal F.\]

The nonnegative forcing follows from \(e\le f\le e+\delta\), which itself implies \(\delta\ge0\).




The smooth admissibility condition and \(m\le L_1\) imply \(0<mh\le1\), so \(q=1-mh\ge0\). The canonical coupling theorem supplies the same forcing for every finite-cost initial coupling. Taking the coupling infimum yields the actual one-step inequality \[W_2(\mu P,\pi)^2\le qW_2(\mu,\pi)^2+\mathcal F.\] Existence of one finite-cost initial coupling proves finiteness of the output distance at the same time; no optimal coupling is chosen.





The reference target is the same at every iteration for a fixed chosen step. One finite input coupling proves finite output transport, while the bound for every coupling gives the recurrence after taking the infimum. The condition \(mh\le1\) ensures a nonnegative recurrence coefficient. The finite potential gap guarantees nonnegative forcing, so each induction step preserves the order of the numerical estimate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Iterate and retain finite moments at every time}{Iterate and retain finite moments at every time}}


Inductively each measurable Markov law is a probability measure and remains at finite distance from the Gibbs reference. If \(\delta\le hK^2/2\), the derived forcing is at most \(mh^2C\). Apply the geometric recurrence with \(q=1-mh\in[0,1)\):

\[W_2(\rho_n,\pi)^2\le(1-mh)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi)^2+hC.\]

The exact denominator is \(1-q=mh>0\).




Induction preserves probability and finite distance to the Gibbs law, allowing the previous one-step theorem to be applied at every time. If \(\delta\le hK^2/2\), the forcing comparison gives \(\mathcal F\le mh^2C\). Thus \(E_{n+1}\le(1-mh)E_n+mh^2C\), and its geometric floor is \((mh^2C)/(mh)=hC\). This establishes \[E_n\le(1-mh)^nE_0+hC.\] The cancellation is legitimate because \(m,h>0\); the resulting squared transport floor is linear in the step.





With \(E_{k+1}\le qE_k+mh^2C\), \(q=1-mh\), geometric summation gives \(E_n\le q^nE_0+mh^2C(1-q^n)/(1-q)\). Since \(1-q=mh>0\), the floor is at most \(hC\). Finite probability laws and transport are maintained through the same induction, so the recurrence can be reapplied at each actual state-law step.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Track the lifted particle coefficient}{Track the lifted particle coefficient}}


Let \(d=d_\theta+Nd_x\). In lifted coordinates substitute \(\bar m=Nm\), \(\bar L=NL_1\), \(\bar K=NK\), \(\sigma=\sqrt{2d}\), and

\[G_N=\sqrt{2(NL_1)^2d/(Nm)+2(NK)^2}+NK.\]

The definition \(C_N=C(Nm,NL_1,G_N,\sqrt{2d},NK)\) is exactly the transport coefficient. Its nonnegativity follows from \(N,m>0\) and the scalar sign lemma.





The lifted smooth score and bounded envelope score both acquire a particle factor \(N\); the Gibbs drift moment uses the full dimension \(D=d_\theta+Nd_x\). Substituting these quantities into the scalar coefficient defines \(C_N\) before the time step is varied. This explicit substitution records all particle dependence and is why independence from the step must not be confused with independence from particle count.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify actual equal-\allowbreak{}step PIPGLA laws}{Identify actual equal-step PIPGLA laws}}


For \(\lambda=\gamma>0\), set \(h=\gamma/N\), \(T=\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\gamma}\), and \(\pi=\Pi_{N,\gamma}\), the Gibbs target of \(g_1+g_2^\gamma\). The averaged proximal energy has the exact required coefficient, and the lifted value gap \(N\gamma K^2/2\) equals \(h(NK)^2/2\). The actual Gaussian algorithm law equals the canonical Markov law. Consequently

\[W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2\le(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N.\]

The coefficient \(C_N\) is independent of \(\gamma\); the reference target itself uses the scale \(\gamma\).






At equal scales, the lifted gap is \(N\gamma K^2/2\). With \(h=\gamma/N\) and \(K_N=NK\), it equals exactly \(hK_N^2/2\), meeting the scalar forcing restriction. The contraction becomes \(1-m\gamma\), and the floor becomes \((\gamma/N)C_N\). The actual Gaussian law identification transfers this bound from canonical transition iterates to the supplied PIPGLA recursion.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Project splitting transport to the original parameter center}{Project splitting transport to the original parameter center}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProjection-lean}


To compare a splitting chain with the original statistical center, first establish its actual finite moments. The parameter projection then converts transport to a smoothed Gibbs reference into RMS, and the finite potential-gap argument moves its center to the original marginal maximizer. The transport distance remains explicit until the endpoint estimate is substituted.


\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Establish state and projected second moments}{Establish state and projected second moments}}


The law equals the pushforward of the constructed \(L^2\) chain. The map-measure criterion then proves \(z\mapsto z\in L^2(\rho_n)\). Apply the bounded linear parameter map and subtract a constant to get \(\theta(z)-a\in L^2(\rho_n)\) for every center \(a\).






The exact law representation as a pushforward of an \(L^2\) iterate gives the state second moment by change of variables. A bounded linear parameter map sends that iterate into \(L^2\), and a deterministic center is an \(L^2\) constant on a probability space. Their difference therefore has finite second moment. These arguments hold for every positive proximal scale before a numerical convergence restriction is imposed.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine transport, equilibrium concentration and center displacement}{Combine transport, equilibrium concentration and center displacement}}


Sharp marginal concentration gives target RMS at most \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\), and the actual uniform potential gap gives \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\). Nonexpansive parameter projection and the center triangle inequality yield

\[\operatorname{RMS}_{\rho_n}(\theta_U)\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}+W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta}).\]

State and target moment bounds establish finite transport before the real-valued projection inequality is used.




Let \(\rho_n\) be the actual PIPGLA state law and \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\) the actual smoothed Gibbs target. Finite state moments make their Wasserstein distance finite. The nonexpansive parameter projection and sharp equilibrium concentration give \[R_{\theta_\eta}(\rho_n)\le W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}.\] Then the center-change lemma and the proved marginal-center shift give \[R_{\theta_U}(\rho_n)\le W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}+\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}.\] This intermediate statement retains the genuine transport distance; the later endpoint theorem supplies its numerical bound.





Use a coupling of the actual chain law and the smoothed target. Parameter projection costs no more than full-state displacement, and the target parameter RMS is at most \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\). Minkowski and the coupling infimum yield the transport-plus-equilibrium estimate. Move the center by \(\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\), proved from the finite smoothing gap. Each term therefore has a separate analytic origin and refers to the same original center.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Sensitivity to the proximal parameter}{Sensitivity to the proximal parameter}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean}


The proximal map is Lipschitz not only in its input but also in its positive scale when the potential is globally value Lipschitz. The single-pair scale estimate passes through particle averaging without a particle loss. Combining it with shared-noise contraction gives a finite-horizon and uniform-in-iteration comparison of actual splitting chains at two proximal scales.




For a convex bounded-below \(K\)-Lipschitz function, the actual minimizer satisfies \(\|P_\lambda(x)-P_\eta(x)\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|\). This feeds a pathwise comparison of PIPGLA chains with different positive proximal parameters.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Bounded supports imply value Lipschitzness}{Bounded supports imply value Lipschitzness}}


Choose a genuine subgradient \(s_x\) at each point with \(\|s_x\|\le K\). Its support at \(y\) gives \(f(x)-f(y)\le\langle s_x,x-y\rangle\le K\|x-y\|\). Reverse \(x,y\) to bound the absolute difference. This derives global Lipschitzness from a bounded section, without differentiability.





At any point with a supporting subgradient of norm at most \(K\), its support bounds the decrease toward another point by \(K\) times the distance. Reversing the roles gives the absolute value bound. Thus a globally bounded genuine subgradient section supplies the value Lipschitz hypothesis used by the scale-sensitivity theorem, without requiring differentiability of the potential.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Compare two actual proximal minimizers}{Compare two actual proximal minimizers}}


Write \(q=P_\eta(x)\) and \(s=(x-q)/\eta\). Actual proximal optimality gives \(s\in\partial f(q)\) and \(\|s\|\le K\). The same support condition shows that \(q\) is also the \(\lambda\)-proximal point of the shifted input \(q+\lambda s\). Nonexpansiveness at scale \(\lambda\) therefore yields

\[\|P_\lambda(x)-q\|\le\|x-(q+\lambda s)\|=|\eta-\lambda|\|s\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

This proves the claimed sensitivity for every pair of positive scales.






Use the residual \(s=(x-P_\eta x)/\eta\) at the second minimizer. The same support makes that minimizer a \(\lambda\)-proximal point at input \(P_\eta x+\lambda s\). Comparing this input with \(x=P_\eta x+\eta s\) by nonexpansiveness yields the scale difference times \(\|s\|\). The uniform residual bound then gives \(K|\lambda-\eta|\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The averaged pair map preserves a uniform map difference}{The averaged pair map preserves a uniform map difference}}


Coordinate linearity proves \(\operatorname{Pair}_{P-Q}=\operatorname{Pair}_P-\operatorname{Pair}_Q\). If \(\sup_x\|P(x)-Q(x)\|\le c\), Jensen bounds the output square by the average of quantities at most \(c^2\), so the output norm is at most \(c\). Apply this with the single-pair proximal sensitivity to obtain the same \(K|\lambda-\eta|\) bound for the actual particle map.




Linearity in the supplied pair map gives \(\operatorname{Pair}(P-Q)=\operatorname{Pair}(P)-\operatorname{Pair}(Q)\). If \(\|P(x)-Q(x)\|\le c\) for all pairs, the averaged squared-distance inequality bounds the resulting state difference by \(N^{-1}\sum_i c^2=c^2\). Taking nonnegative square roots proves the same uniform \(c\), with no particle-number loss. Substitute \(c=K|\lambda-\eta|\) from the previous proximal sensitivity estimate.





Apply the averaged-square inequality to the difference of the two pair maps. Every single-pair difference is bounded by \(c\), so the state square is at most \(N^{-1}\sum_i c^2=c^2\). Taking a nonnegative square root gives the same constant \(c\). Substituting the scale-sensitivity bound therefore preserves its coefficient despite averaging a common parameter and rescaling latent outputs.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Solve the pathwise parameter-\allowbreak{}perturbation recursion}{Solve the pathwise parameter-perturbation recursion}}


Insert an intermediate update with the same parameter, then combine shared-noise contraction and the uniform parameter difference:

\[e_{n+1}\le(1-m\gamma)e_n+K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

Induction yields \(e_n\le(1-m\gamma)^ne_0+K|\lambda-\eta|\sum_{j<n}(1-m\gamma)^j\). For \(\gamma>0\), the admissible contraction has \(0\le1-m\gamma<1\), so its sum is at most \(1/(m\gamma)\). These bounds hold for every supplied innovation path.




Insert an intermediate update with input \(w\) and proximal parameter \(\lambda\). The triangle inequality separates the state change from the parameter change, giving \[e_{k+1}\le(1-m\gamma)e_k+K|\lambda-\eta|.\] The finite-horizon theorem sums this derived pathwise recurrence, while \(\gamma>0\) gives the strict factor \(1-m\gamma<1\) needed for the floor \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). If the two runs start together, the initial term vanishes. No independence hypothesis is needed: both runs use the same arbitrary innovation sequence.





Insert an intermediate update using the first proximal scale but the second current state. Shared-noise contraction bounds the state change by \(q e_k\), and uniform map sensitivity bounds the scale change by \(K|\lambda-\eta|\). Sum the resulting recurrence. For \(q=1-m\gamma<1\), its floor is \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\), and a common initialization removes the transient term.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A proximal corridor gives an explicit scalar bound}{A proximal corridor gives an explicit scalar bound}}


If \(0<m\gamma<1\) and \(\gamma\le\lambda\le\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\), then \(|\lambda-\gamma|=\lambda-\gamma\le m\gamma^2/(1-m\gamma)\). Divide by the positive \(m\gamma\) and multiply by \(K\) to get \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\le K\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\). This is a scalar consequence of the true sensitivity theorem.






The corridor width is \(\gamma/(1-m\gamma)-\gamma=m\gamma^2/(1-m\gamma)\). Since its lower endpoint is \(\gamma\), the absolute scale difference is nonnegative and bounded by this width. Dividing by \(m\gamma>0\) yields the displayed comparison cost. The strict condition \(m\gamma<1\) is necessary for its finite denominator.

\section{\texorpdfstring{PIPGLA about the original center for every positive proximal scale}{PIPGLA about the original center for every positive proximal scale}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTheorem-lean}


The equal-step PIPGLA transport theorem supplies a convergent reference estimate with finite coefficient. Pathwise sensitivity compares any positive proximal scale with that equal-step chain. Projection and the finite smoothing displacement then give the original-center RMS bound for the actual splitting algorithm, with the scale-mismatch cost shown explicitly.




Assume the same strongly convex smooth \(g_1\), convex bounded-below \(K\)-Lipschitz \(g_2\), \(N>0\), \(0<m\le L_1\), \(\lambda,\gamma>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_1)\). Initialization is in \(L^2\) and the actual coordinate Gaussian innovations satisfy joint independence.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Convert the pathwise scale comparison into RMS}{Convert the pathwise scale comparison into RMS}}


Drive the \(\lambda\)- and \(\eta\)-chains by the same initial variable and innovations. Their state distance is at most \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). Both chains have finite second moments. The parameter projection is nonexpansive, so Minkowski gives

\[\operatorname{RMS}_\lambda(a)\le\operatorname{RMS}_\eta(a)+\frac{K|\lambda-\eta|}{m\gamma}.\]

The proof works with the actual \(L^2\) realizations and then identifies their pushforward integrals.




Here is the conversion from a pathwise comparison to the two displayed law integrals. On the supplied probability space let \(X\) and \(Y\) be the actual \(L^2\) PIPGLA iterates with proximal parameters \(\lambda\) and \(\eta\), the same initialization, and the same innovations. The pathwise parameter-sensitivity theorem gives

\[\|X(\omega)-Y(\omega)\|\le c:=\frac{K|\lambda-\eta|}{m\gamma}\quad\text{almost surely}.\]



The pathwise bound is uniform over the probability source, so its \(L^2\) norm is at most the same constant because the source mass is one. Projection cannot increase that norm. Adding the equal-reference-center error by Minkowski therefore gives exactly the displayed RMS comparison. Both laws are represented by actual square-integrable chain iterates, ensuring the square-root integrals are finite.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Combine equal-\allowbreak{}step transport and original-\allowbreak{}center projection}{Combine equal-step transport and original-center projection}}


Set the comparison scale to \(\eta=\gamma\). The equal-step law theorem and the original-center projection inequality give

\[\begin{aligned}\operatorname{RMS}_\lambda(\theta_U)\le{}&\frac{K|\lambda-\gamma|}{m\gamma}+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\&+\sqrt{(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N}.\end{aligned}\]

Here \(C_N\) is the explicit finite coefficient defined in the transport estimate. It is independent of the time step. Every positive \(\lambda\) is covered, with the displayed parameter-comparison cost; a vanishing error as \(\gamma\to0\) additionally requires controlling that cost. This is the proved replacement rate, not the paper’s printed heat-flow constants.




The equal-step transport theorem controls the actual law at proximal scale \(\gamma\) (with the lifted smoothing scale prescribed in its statement). The projection theorem adds the sharp equilibrium RMS and the original-center displacement. To handle a different positive proximal parameter \(\lambda\), compare the two pointwise runs under the same innovations: their uniform difference is at most \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\). Integrating this deterministic bound yields the same RMS difference because the probability has mass one. The final result adds this scale-comparison term to the already proved equal-step error; the parameter corridor then supplies its explicit simpler upper bound.





Take the reference proximal scale equal to the physical forward step. Its squared transport bound gives a square root of the transient-plus-floor expression. Projection adds sharp equilibrium spread and original-center smoothing displacement. Finally, scale sensitivity adds \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\). Covering all positive \(\lambda\) does not imply a uniform vanishing small-step bound unless this extra mismatch term is controlled as the step decreases.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Gaussian displacement and frozen-\allowbreak{}gradient error energy}{Gaussian displacement and frozen-gradient error energy}}
\label{proof-module-UnadjustedLangevin-LSI-GaussianDrift-lean}


For \(Y_t=X-t\nabla U(X)+\sqrt{2t}Z\), independent Gaussian noise makes the cross term vanish, giving \(\mathbb E\|Y_t-X\|^2=t^2\mathbb E\|\nabla U(X)\|^2+2td\). Gradient Lipschitzness bounds the corresponding frozen-gradient error by \(L^2\) times this moment.



Put \(G_0=\mathbb E\|\nabla U(X)\|^2\) and let \(Z\) be an independent standard Gaussian. The displacement is \(-t\nabla U(X)+\sqrt{2t}Z\), whose squared mean is \(t^2G_0+2td\): the mixed term vanishes by centering and independence. Gradient Lipschitzness multiplies this energy by at most \(L^2\). This controls drift error without assuming \(\mathbb E\|X\|^2<\infty\), since the absolute initial position cancels from the displacement.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The standard Gaussian second moment}{The standard Gaussian second moment}}


Choose a finite orthonormal basis and use \(\|Z\|^2=\sum_i\langle e_i,Z\rangle^2\). Each standard Gaussian coordinate has second moment one, proved using its dual variance formula and zero mean. Gaussian \(L^2\) membership justifies integrating the finite sum, giving \(\mathbb E\|Z\|^2=d\), also when \(d=0\).



The Gaussian covariance is the identity, so every orthonormal coordinate contributes one to the energy. Summing the finite coordinate squares gives \(d\), without any trace normalization factor. This is the source of the dimension term in the drift-error estimate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The energy of an affine Gaussian displacement}{The energy of an affine Gaussian displacement}}


Expand \(\|ag(X)+bZ\|^2=a^2\|g(X)\|^2+2ab\langle g(X),Z\rangle+b^2\|Z\|^2\). The \(L^2\) hypothesis on \(g\) and Gaussian \(L^2\) membership prove all three terms integrable on the independent product.



The product bilinear-integral theorem factors the cross expectation as \(\langle\mathbb Eg(X),\mathbb EZ\rangle=0\). The product marginals identify the first energy, and the second moment \(\mathbb E\|Z\|^2=d\) identifies the noise energy. Hence the integral is \(a^2\mathbb E\|g\|^2+b^2d\).



The mixed term is integrable by Cauchy–Schwarz and independence factors its expectation. Since the Gaussian mean is zero, that expectation vanishes even when the drift has nonzero mean. The drift and Gaussian square terms then contribute their exact separate energies.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Subtracting the initial state from the proposal}{Subtracting the initial state from the proposal}}


Subtract \(x\) from \(T_t(x,z)=x-t\nabla U(x)+\sqrt{2t}z\). Additive cancellation leaves \(-t\nabla U(x)+\sqrt{2t}z\). This algebraic identity shows why a state second moment is unnecessary when only displacement energy is estimated.



The starting position cancels algebraically before integration. Thus its absolute second moment is irrelevant to this displacement identity; the only initial moment required is that of its gradient. This distinction permits finite-KL initial laws without an added position-moment assumption.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The exact frozen-\allowbreak{}drift displacement energy}{The exact frozen-drift displacement energy}}


Apply the affine Gaussian energy formula with \(a=-t\), \(b=\sqrt{2t}\), and \(g=\nabla U\). For \(t\ge0\), \(b^2=2t\), yielding \(\mathbb E\|T_t-X\|^2=t^2\mathbb E\|\nabla U(X)\|^2+2td\). Independence and centering have already discharged the cross term.



With \(a=-t\) and \(b=\sqrt{2t}\), the squared coefficients are \(t^2\) and \(2t\). The exact energy identity therefore has no extra factor from bounding the cross term, unlike a triangle-inequality estimate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The frozen-\allowbreak{}gradient error energy}{The frozen-gradient error energy}}


Lipschitzness gives \(\|\nabla U(T_t)-\nabla U(X)\|^2\le L^2\|T_t-X\|^2\) pointwise. Convert integrability of the squared gradient to \(L^2\) membership, so the displacement has an integrable squared norm.



Continuity of the proposal and gradient proves measurability of the squared discrepancy. Domination by the integrable displacement energy proves its integrability; monotonicity of the integral and the exact displacement moment then give \(L^2(t^2G_0+2td)\). This is a joint-law estimate; a state-space conditional drift is constructed separately.



Apply gradient Lipschitzness pointwise to the actual state displacement, then square and integrate. The resulting energy is \(L^2(t^2G_0+2td)\), retaining the sharp independent-Gaussian constant in the dimensional term.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Square-\allowbreak{}integrability of the displacement}{Square-integrability of the displacement}}


Pull the square-integrable initial gradient to the first product coordinate and the square-integrable Gaussian identity to the second. Their scalar multiples and sum lie in \(L^2\). The displacement identity rewrites this sum as \(T_t(X,Z)-X\).



The drift term is square-integrable by the initial score hypothesis and the Gaussian term by its second moment. Their finite linear combination is consequently square-integrable, which validates the preceding exact norm-square calculation.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Orthogonal masks and the exact removed-\allowbreak{}noise cost}{Orthogonal masks and the exact removed-noise cost}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDNoise-lean}


Removing parameter noise can be compared exactly with retaining full Gaussian noise by using orthogonal coordinate masks. Independence of a fresh centered Gaussian makes the mixed squared-error term vanish. The additional mean-square cost is exactly the removed noise variance, involving parameter dimension and no latent dimension.




Let \(P_\theta\) retain the common parameter block of the scaled particle state, and \(P_x=I-P_\theta\) retain the latent blocks. For a standard Gaussian vector \(\xi\), the removed parameter noise has mean zero and second moment \(d_\theta\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define the actual complementary coordinate projections}{Define the actual complementary coordinate projections}}


Set \(P_\theta(\theta,x)=(\theta,0)\) and \(P_x(\theta,x)=(0,x)\). Coordinate evaluation proves the four block identities by unfolding the definitions. These are bounded linear maps, so masking an \(L^2\) innovation remains an actual \(L^2\) random variable.






The masks satisfy \(P_\theta+P_x=I\), \(P_\theta P_x=0\), and \(P_\theta^2=P_\theta\), \(P_x^2=P_x\). Thus their ranges are the orthogonal parameter and latent subspaces. Applying \(P_x\) changes only the innovation block; it leaves the deterministic drift map untouched. This is the geometry needed to compare MYPGD with its full-noise counterpart.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Compute the projection geometry and Gaussian moments}{Compute the projection geometry and Gaussian moments}}


The Euclidean square is \(\|P_\theta z\|^2=\sum_{j=1}^{d_\theta}z_j^2\), hence \(\|P_\theta z\|\le\|z\|\). A bounded linear map commutes with the Gaussian expectation, giving \(\mathbb E P_\theta\xi=0\). Each retained standard coordinate has second moment one; finite summation gives \(\mathbb E\|P_\theta\xi\|^2=d_\theta\).




The squared norm expands to \(\sum_{j=1}^{d_\theta}z_j^2\), with no latent terms. Every coordinate square is integrable because the standard Gaussian identity map belongs to \(L^2\). The coordinate variance identity gives \(\mathbb E z_j^2=1\) and the continuous-linear-map integration identity gives \(\mathbb E P_\theta Z=P_\theta\mathbb EZ=0\). Interchanging only a finite sum and the integral yields \(\mathbb E\|P_\theta Z\|^2=d_\theta\). No factor \(N\) belongs in this dimension count.




The projection keeps exactly \(d_\theta\) independent standard scalar coordinates. Their squares each have expectation one, so summing gives \(d_\theta\). The expectation is zero by linearity of the projection and centering of the full Gaussian. The moment calculation does not require assigning a particle noise variance to these coordinates: the particle factor enters later through the amplitude \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The product law cancels the mixed term exactly}{The product law cancels the mixed term exactly}}


For any square-integrable \(g\) on a probability space independent of \(\xi\), expand \(\|g+bP_\theta\xi\|^2\). Fubini and bilinearity factor its mixed expectation as \(\langle\mathbb E g,\mathbb E P_\theta\xi\rangle=0\). Thus

\[\mathbb E\|g+bP_\theta\xi\|^2=\mathbb E\|g\|^2+b^2d_\theta.\]

The proof establishes integrability of every term before distributing the integral.






Independence concerns the fresh Gaussian and the preexisting discrepancy \(g\). It does not require \(g\) itself to be centered. In the squared expansion, Fubini factors \(\mathbb E\langle g,P_\theta\xi\rangle\) as the pairing of the two expectations; the second is zero. Consequently adding or subtracting the removed block has exactly the same variance cost \(b^2d_\theta\), without a triangle-inequality loss.

\section{\texorpdfstring{A concrete latent/\allowbreak{}full Gaussian transition coupling}{A concrete latent/full Gaussian transition coupling}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTransport-lean}


A shared Gaussian vector couples the latent-only and full-noise transitions. The discrepancy splits into a deterministic contracted input difference and the omitted parameter noise. The exact variance identity bounds the output coupling cost, and finite-moment propagation ensures this is a coupling of genuine probability laws with finite quadratic transport.




For an \(r\)-Lipschitz deterministic map \(D\), compare \(D(x)+aP_x\xi\) and \(D(y)+a\xi\) using the same fresh standard Gaussian vector. Initial laws are probability measures with finite second moments.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Lipschitz maps and explicit pairs have finite transport cost}{Lipschitz maps and explicit pairs have finite transport cost}}


Center \(D\) at zero to use \(\|D(x)-D(0)\|\le r\|x\|\), then add back its constant value; this proves preservation of \(L^2\). For two \(L^2\) variables on one space, their joint law is a coupling. Its cost bounds \(W_2\), yielding both finiteness and \(W_2(\mathcal L(X),\mathcal L(Y))^2\le\mathbb E\|X-Y\|^2\).






A Lipschitz map has affine linear growth, so a finite-second-moment input gives a finite-second-moment output even when the map is not zero at the origin. For a joint realization \((X,Y)\), its pushforward to the product state is an admissible coupling. Thus \(W_2\) is bounded by its root mean-square separation directly from the defining infimum, and finiteness follows at the same time.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurable Gaussian maps preserve second moments}{Measurable Gaussian maps preserve second moments}}


The two maps are \(T_x(u,\xi)=D(u)+aP_x\xi\) and \(T(u,\xi)=D(u)+a\xi\). Lipschitz continuity of \(D\), linearity of \(P_x\), addition and scalar multiplication prove joint measurability. On \(\mu\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\), each summand belongs to \(L^2\), so both pushforward transition laws have finite second moments.






Under the product law, the drift term depends only on the input coordinate and the Gaussian term only on the new noise coordinate. Each has finite second moment; the squared norm of their sum is at most twice the sum of their squares. This establishes finite output moments and permits the exact mixed-term calculation in the next step. Measurability of all maps follows from their continuity and linear coordinate operations.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Extend every input coupling by an independent Gaussian}{Extend every input coupling by an independent Gaussian}}


For any input coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), work on \(c\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\). The two outputs have precisely the required Markov marginals, and their difference is \(D(x)-D(y)-aP_\theta\xi\). The exact removed-noise identity gives squared coupling cost \(\mathbb E_c\|D(x)-D(y)\|^2+a^2d_\theta\), bounded by \(r^2\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+a^2d_\theta\). This constructs the recurrence input, with no optimal-coupling assumption.






The coupling's first output marginal uses input law \(\mu\) and masked Gaussian noise; its second uses \(\nu\) and full Gaussian noise. Their common source preserves both required transition laws. The difference is \(D(X)-D(Y)-aP_\theta\xi\). Independence removes its cross term, and the Lipschitz inequality gives output mean square at most \(r^2\mathbb E\|X-Y\|^2+a^2d_\theta\). This bound holds for every finite input coupling.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Iterated finite-\allowbreak{}particle noise-\allowbreak{}removal comparison}{Iterated finite-particle noise-removal comparison}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGD-lean}


The one-step noise-removal coupling yields a squared Wasserstein recurrence between the actual latent-only and full-noise chains. Finite moments and probability mass propagate through both transitions. From a common initialization, geometric summation bounds their finite-particle separation, and the true smoothed forward contraction supplies an explicit parameter-dimension floor.




Finite-moment latent-only and full Gaussian chains use the same deterministic \(r\)-contractive map \(D\) and amplitude \(a\). Their actual Markov laws are compared at every iteration.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Pass from every input coupling to actual transport}{Pass from every input coupling to actual transport}}


The preceding coupling supplies \(W_2(T_x\mu,T\nu)^2\le r^2\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+a^2d_\theta\) for every finite input coupling. Finite second moments prove \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\), so the infimum lemma yields \(W_2(T_x\mu,T\nu)^2\le r^2W_2(\mu,\nu)^2+a^2d_\theta\).




The preceding estimate holds for every finite-cost input coupling, with the same additive \(a^2d_\theta\). Finite second moments first ensure \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\). The infimum lemma then gives \[W_2(\mu P_x,\nu P)^2\le r^2W_2(\mu,\nu)^2+a^2d_\theta.\] The existence of one finite-cost coupling also proves finiteness of the output distance. An optimal coupling is unnecessary: the argument uses a common bound for the entire family of admissible couplings.





Because the same upper bound holds for every input coupling, taking its cost infimum replaces the coupling square by \(W_2(\mu,\nu)^2\). The coefficient \(r^2\) is nonnegative, so this operation preserves order. Finite input moments ensure that the infimum is finite, and one finite coupling already supplies a finite output cost. No optimal coupling needs to be chosen.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Iterate from a common initialization}{Iterate from a common initialization}}


Measurability preserves probability mass, and the one-step moment lemmas preserve \(L^2\) by induction. Since both chains start from \(\mu\), their initial transport distance is zero. For \(0\le r<1\), the geometric recurrence gives \(W_2(\rho_n^x,\rho_n)^2\le a^2d_\theta/(1-r^2)\), along with finiteness at every time.






Starting from the same initial law gives zero initial distance via the diagonal coupling. Induction on \(E_{k+1}\le r^2E_k+a^2d_\theta\) gives \(E_k\le a^2d_\theta\sum_{j<k}r^{2j}\). The strict condition \(r<1\) makes the denominator \(1-r^2\) positive and bounds the finite sum by its infinite geometric value. The estimate is uniform over finite iteration counts.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The genuine smoothed forward map contracts}{The genuine smoothed forward map contracts}}


The common deterministic smoothed step contracts by \(r=1-m\gamma\) when \(0<m\le L_\eta\) and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\). Couple any two current laws, share their latent Gaussian noise, and use the same full Gaussian vector for the MYIPLA step. The exact noise identity proves the actual one-step squared transport estimate


\[
W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_{k+1},\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_{k+1})^2
 \le r^2 W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_k,\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_k)^2
       +\frac{2\gamma d_\theta}{N}.
\]


The proof constructs each output coupling, establishes its finite moments, and takes the infimum over input couplings. Starting from the same initial law, \(0\le r<1\) and \(1-r^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\ge m\gamma\) give


\[
W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_n,\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_n)^2
 \le\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}.
\]


This is a conservative comparison radius, not a claim that removing parameter noise worsens the algorithm. The estimate follows from the two actual recursions and their genuine independent Gaussian transitions.




For \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), the earlier Moreau results give differentiability, strong convexity \(m\) and score Lipschitz constant \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). Lifting gives constants \(Nm,NL_\eta\), and the Euler step \(\gamma/N\) consequently contracts with \(r=1-m\gamma\). The admissible step \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\), with \(m\le L_\eta\), proves \(0\le m\gamma\le1\), hence \(0\le r<1\).





With \(r=1-m\gamma\) and \(a^2=2\gamma/N\), the denominator is \(1-r^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\). The admissible step implies \(m\gamma\le1\), so this denominator is at least \(m\gamma>0\). Canceling the step yields the conservative squared radius \(2d_\theta/(Nm)\). This is a comparison between two finite-particle algorithms, not a claim of a mean-field limit.

\section{\texorpdfstring{MYPGD pointwise recursion and its exact Markov law}{MYPGD pointwise recursion and its exact Markov law}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDAlgorithm-lean}


MYPGD keeps the smoothed proximal-residual drift of MYIPLA and removes only its parameter Gaussian block. Exact update identities identify its pointwise chain with the corresponding latent-noise Euler recursion. The complete Gaussian path assumptions then identify its law with a canonical Markov iteration, permitting the noise-removal estimate to be applied to the actual realization.




MYPGD is the expanded MYIPLA drift with only latent Gaussian noise retained. The definitions use actual pairwise proximity and independent smoothing and time parameters.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Mask only the innovations in the pointwise chain}{Mask only the innovations in the pointwise chain}}


Define the update by replacing \(\xi\) in MYIPLA with \(P_x\xi\). The zero and successor recursion identities show inductively that the resulting MYPGD chain equals the MYIPLA pointwise chain driven by \((P_x\xi_k)_k\), and equals the generic noise-driven chain for its own update. Its law is the pushforward of this actual chain.





Apply the latent mask to every supplied innovation before evaluating the MYIPLA update. The deterministic terms are unchanged at every iteration. Equality at time zero and equality of the two successor maps prove the chain identity by induction. The resulting law is still defined from the explicit state recursion; the masked path is an input construction, not an assumed distributional approximation.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Recognize the shared deterministic map}{Recognize the shared deterministic map}}


Let \(F_\eta=\sum_i(g_1+g_2^\eta)(\theta,x_i)\) in scaled coordinates and \(h=\gamma/N\). The displayed MYPGD step is exactly


\[
X_{k+1}=X_k-h\nabla F_\eta(X_k)+\sqrt{2h}\,P_x\xi_k.
\]


Its common parameter coordinate has no Gaussian perturbation; each physical latent coordinate has the usual \(\sqrt{2\gamma}\) noise. The expanded proximal-residual formula is identified with this gradient step. The pointwise chain is then identified with its measurable Gaussian Markov recursion.



Initialization has finite second moment, and the standard Gaussian vectors are independent across coordinates and times and independent of initialization. The parameter coordinates of those vectors are erased in MYPGD. They remain available as auxiliary noise for the comparison with MYIPLA. No initial iid-particle hypothesis is needed for this weaker finite-particle theorem.



The auxiliary measurability estimate uses the unconditional bound \(\operatorname{Lip}(I-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_\eta)\le1+|\gamma/N|\,NL_\eta\). It needs no contraction restriction and supplies measurable updates even before the convergence regime is imposed.





The residual-envelope identity gives \(D(z)=z-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_\eta(z)\). Its parameter output has no random additive term, while the unscaled latent output has coefficient \(\sqrt{2\gamma}\). Global Lipschitzness is enough to establish a measurable finite-moment recursion before imposing contraction. The stronger step restriction is used only in the quantitative comparison theorem.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Independent coordinate Gaussians give the Markov recursion}{Independent coordinate Gaussians give the Markov recursion}}


The Gaussian-innovation hypotheses imply that vector innovations have standard Gaussian law, are independent over time, and are independent of initialization. The measurable-chain law theorem therefore identifies the two pushforwards with the actual iterated Markov transitions \(D+aP_x\xi\) and \(D+a\xi\). This transfers coupling estimates from the canonical product space to any admissible algorithm realization.




The two update identities recognize the same deterministic map \(D(z)=z-(\gamma/N)\nabla V_{N,\eta}(z)\). MYPGD adds \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}P_x\xi\), while MYIPLA adds the full \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\xi\). The coordinate-Gaussian assumptions give the complete independent standard vector-noise path and independence from initialization. The shared iid-chain law theorem consequently identifies each supplied realization with its iterated Markov transition. This equality is needed before the abstract Markov comparison can be cited for these algorithms.





At time \(k\), the current state is a measurable function of initialization and earlier innovations. Independence of the full Gaussian path makes the next vector fresh relative to that information. Applying the mask gives exactly the latent Gaussian transition, while keeping it gives the full MYIPLA transition. Equality with their canonical Markov iterates transfers the transport comparison to any realization with these same input assumptions.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Actual MYPGD bound about the original marginal center}{Actual MYPGD bound about the original marginal center}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTheorem-lean}


The original-center MYPGD theorem adds a proved noise-removal radius to the corresponding MYIPLA estimate. All laws are identified with their actual Gaussian recursions. The resulting five contributions are the comparison radius, finite smoothing displacement, equilibrium spread, initial relaxation and smoothed Euler discretization.




Under the MYIPLA primitive assumptions, also assume \(m\le L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). The algorithm uses genuine latent-only Gaussian updates; \(N>0\), \(m,\eta,\gamma>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify actual algorithm laws in the comparison}{Identify actual algorithm laws in the comparison}}


Rewrite both output laws as the Gaussian Markov recursions of the shared smoothed drift. The initialization pushforward has finite second moment because its representative belongs to \(L^2\). The iterated noise-removal theorem now proves finite transport distance and \(W_2(\rho_n^{\rm PGD},\rho_n^{\rm IPLA})^2\le2d_\theta/(Nm)\).





Both chains have the same initial pushforward and deterministic smoothed forward map. The Gaussian path law and its independence identify them with the two Markov transitions analyzed earlier. The noise-removal theorem therefore proves a finite transport comparison for their actual output laws. Its squared upper bound is \(2d_\theta/(Nm)\), with every second moment established from the recursion rather than assumed from the desired rate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Transfer parameter RMS through a nonexpansive projection}{Transfer parameter RMS through a nonexpansive projection}}


The parameter map has operator norm at most one. The projected RMS transport inequality yields \(\operatorname{RMS}_{\rm PGD}(\theta)\le W_2(\rho_n^{\rm PGD},\rho_n^{\rm IPLA})+\operatorname{RMS}_{\rm IPLA}(\theta)\). MYIPLA finite moments and the finite comparison imply the MYPGD moment is finite before passing to real square-root integrals. Taking the square root of the comparison gives the added radius \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\).




Finite second moments of both algorithm laws give finite transport. Projecting a coupling onto the common parameter, whose operator norm is at most one, gives \[R_{\mathrm{MYPGD}}(\theta_*)\le R_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(\theta_*)+W_2(\rho_n^{\mathrm{MYPGD}},\rho_n^{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}).\] The actual-law comparison bounds the squared last term by \(2d_\theta/(Nm)\); nonnegative square roots therefore bound it by \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\). The final theorem inserts the already proved original-center MYIPLA estimate, retaining its smoothing, equilibrium and discretization contributions.





Project a coupling of the two algorithm laws onto the common parameter. The projection cannot increase its quadratic cost. Adding the MYIPLA parameter error about the desired center and using Minkowski gives the MYPGD RMS bound. Taking the infimum over couplings gives the Wasserstein term, and its squared comparison estimate gives \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\). Finite moments justify every square-root expectation involved.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Insert the proved original-\allowbreak{}center MYIPLA theorem}{Insert the proved original-center MYIPLA theorem}}


Apply the parameter-projection transport inequality to the two actual laws, then use the proved MYIPLA original-center theorem. With \(L_\eta=L_1+1/\eta\) and the assumptions above,


\[
\begin{aligned}
\left(\mathbb E\|\theta^{\mathrm{PGD}}_n-\theta_\star\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\le{}&\sqrt{\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}}
 +\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\
&+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta})\\
&+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}
 \sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.
\end{aligned}
\]


Every term is finite under the primitive assumptions, and every law in the statement is the actual defined algorithm law. This is a genuine finite-particle replacement for Appendix B’s Theorem B.1. It has a Gibbs initial-law transient and an explicit conservative comparison term; it does not reproduce the paper’s mean-field metric, constants, or stronger Taylor smoothing expression.





The MYIPLA bound already refers to the original marginal maximizer and includes its finite smoothing displacement. Adding the comparison radius leaves its other terms unchanged. The initial transient remains measured against the smoothed Gibbs reference, and the discretization coefficient retains \(L_1+1/\eta\). The result is therefore a finite-particle bound with explicit dependence on the smoothing scale, distinct from a stronger path-supremum or mean-field estimate.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The actual mean-\allowbreak{}field force is Lipschitz in W\ensuremath{{}_{2}}}{The actual mean-field force is Lipschitz in W₂}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldDrift-lean}


The mean-field parameter force is the actual integral of the parameter block of the joint gradient. Linear growth from global gradient Lipschitzness makes this integral well defined for finite-second-moment latent laws. Comparing integrands on arbitrary couplings gives a Wasserstein continuity estimate with the correct split-envelope regularity constant.




For a pair potential \(V\) with \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, define \(b_V(\theta,\mu)=\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\mu(dx)\). The measures are probability laws with finite second moments. Every Bochner expectation and every finite real transport distance is justified.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A Lipschitz observable preserves L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} and controls expectation differences}{A Lipschitz observable preserves L² and controls expectation differences}}


Center an observable \(\phi\) at \(\phi(0)\); its norm growth is bounded by \(K\|x\|\), so \(\phi(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\). Given any finite-cost coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), pushforward integration and Cauchy–Schwarz give

\[\left\|\int\phi\,d\mu-\int\phi\,d\nu\right\|\le K\left(\int\|x-y\|^2dc\right)^{1/2}.\]

Taking the actual coupling infimum yields the bound \(K W_2(\mu,\nu)\), with finiteness supplied by the second moments.


\[\int\varphi\,d\mu-\int\varphi\,d\nu=\int[\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)]\,dc(x,y).\]



A Lipschitz observable can have a nonzero value at the origin, so its integrability estimate must include that constant. After establishing integrability, write the expectation difference as a single coupling integral. The observable's Lipschitz constant bounds its integrand by the pair distance. The probability-space inequality \(\|\cdot\|_1\le\|\cdot\|_2\) then bounds it by quadratic coupling cost, and taking the infimum gives the Wasserstein estimate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Project the true joint gradient to its parameter block}{Project the true joint gradient to its parameter block}}


The bounded linear map \(P_\theta\) keeps the parameter coordinates. Its squared norm is a sub-sum of the full Euclidean square, hence \(\|P_\theta z\|\le\|z\|\). Define \(\phi_\theta(x)=P_\theta\nabla V(\theta,x)\), then define the mean-field force as its actual Bochner integral. These definitions use the existing joint potential, with no arbitrary force field substituted for its gradient.





Orthogonal parameter projection cannot increase the joint gradient difference. Thus the parameter-gradient observable inherits the joint gradient's Lipschitz and affine-growth bounds. Integrating this actual block, rather than a separately postulated force, fixes the mean-field equation's relationship to the underlying potential. Its expectation exists because finite latent second moment also gives finite first moment.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Control joint parameter and latent perturbations}{Control joint parameter and latent perturbations}}


Decompose a joint pair difference into its two coordinate inclusions, giving \(\|(\theta,x)-(\theta',x')\|\le\|\theta-\theta'\|+\|x-x'\|\). Gradient Lipschitzness and the nonexpansive parameter projection imply

\[\|\phi_\theta(x)-\phi_{\theta'}(x')\|\le L(\|\theta-\theta'\|+\|x-x'\|).\]

Fixing \(\theta=\theta'\) proves that the latent observable itself is \(L\)-Lipschitz.




Decompose the pair difference into a pure parameter displacement and a pure latent displacement. Their orthogonal inclusions have norm one; the triangle inequality gives their sum as an upper bound for the joint norm. Applying the joint score Lipschitz estimate and then parameter projection yields the displayed two-block estimate. Fixing either block gives the corresponding separate regularity bound.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Separate the parameter change from the law change}{Separate the parameter change from the law change}}


For a differentiable potential \(V\), define


\[
b_V(\theta,\mu)=\int \nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\,\mu(dx).
\]


Assume \(\nabla V\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz and \(\mu,\nu\) are probability laws with finite second moment. Coordinate projection has norm at most one, so the integrand is Lipschitz and grows at most linearly. Its Bochner expectation is therefore integrable. For any finite-cost coupling, compare its two integrands, apply the norm-of-integral inequality, and use Cauchy–Schwarz. Taking the infimum of actual coupling costs gives


\[
\|b_V(\theta,\mu)-b_V(\theta',\nu)\|
 \le L\bigl(\|\theta-\theta'\|+W_2(\mu,\nu)\bigr).
\]


With the genuine split envelope \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\), the proved constant is \(L_1+1/\eta\). The paper’s \(1/\eta\) constant applies to a full envelope, not to this split expression in general. This is a corrected \textbf{W\ensuremath{{}_{2}} version} of Lemma C.1: its right-hand side is weaker than the paper’s W\ensuremath{{}_{1}} statement, and the distinction is explicit. Propositions C.2–C.3 are not consequences formalized by this lemma alone.




Fix an arbitrary coupling \((X,Y)\) of the two latent laws. Split the force difference into a parameter change at fixed law and a law change at fixed parameter. Nonexpansive parameter projection and the full score's \(L\)-Lipschitz property give \(\|\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)-\nabla_\theta U(\vartheta,y)\|\le L(\|\theta-\vartheta\|+\|x-y\|)\). Integrability of this observable follows from the latent second moments. Integrate, use Cauchy–Schwarz on \(\mathbb E\|X-Y\|\), and take the coupling infimum to obtain \[\|b(\theta,\mu)-b(\vartheta,\nu)\|\le L\|\theta-\vartheta\|+LW_2(\mu,\nu).\] The Moreau specialization substitutes the already proved score constant \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\).





Add and subtract \(b_V(\theta',\mu)\). The parameter change is bounded by \(L\|\theta-\theta'\|\), using the same latent law and source mass one. The law change is bounded by \(LW_2(\mu,\nu)\), using arbitrary couplings. Adding yields the result. For split Moreau smoothing, both scores contribute, so its constant is \(L_1+1/\eta\), rather than the full-envelope constant alone.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Construct the mean-\allowbreak{}field L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} integral equations}{Construct the mean-field L² integral equations}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean}


A mean-field process can be constructed as an ordinary integral equation in the complete space of square-integrable random joint states. A bounded linear averaging operator replaces only the parameter score by its expectation. Lipschitzness of the actual gradient permits Picard construction, and projection then derives a deterministic parameter path coupled to the actual latent law.




Work on a probability space \(Q\), with \(H=L^2(Q;\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x})\). A potential \(V\) with globally \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient defines the actual mean-field drift. Its continuous finite-horizon integral solution is constructed by the Banach-space Picard theorem.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Expectation is a bounded linear map from L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{Expectation is a bounded linear map from L²}}


The norm-of-integral inequality and \(\|X\|_{L^1}\le\|X\|_{L^2}\) on a probability space give \(\|\mathbb E_Q X\|\le\|X\|_2\). Bochner additivity and scalar linearity therefore define a bounded linear map \(\mathcal E:H\to E\) with operator norm at most one. The evaluation lemma records that its value is the actual integral.





Bochner integration is linear on square-integrable random variables, since probability normalization implies integrability. The norm bound follows from the norm-of-integral inequality followed by Cauchy–Schwarz. Thus expectation is continuous with norm at most one. As a bounded linear map, it commutes with later continuous time integrals, which is needed when extracting the deterministic parameter equation.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Coordinate projections and a parameter inclusion}{Coordinate projections and a parameter inclusion}}


Define \(P_x(\theta,x)=x\) and \(I_\theta(\theta)=(\theta,0)\). Their coordinate identities follow by evaluating the definitions. The Euclidean square identity \(\|(\theta,0)\|^2=\|\theta\|^2\), followed by nonnegative square-root cancellation, proves that \(I_\theta\) is an isometry.





The parameter inclusion inserts zeros in all latent coordinates. The latent projection forgets the parameter coordinates. Expanding the Euclidean square shows that the inclusion preserves norms and the projection does not increase them. These coordinate maps act pointwise on square-integrable variables and retain their bounded linearity, giving precise control of the projected integral equations.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A true latent mask erases all parameter coordinates}{A true latent mask erases all parameter coordinates}}


Set \(M_x=I-I_\theta P_\theta\). Its parameter block is zero and its latent block is unchanged. Expanding the Euclidean squared norm shows \(\|M_xz\|^2\le\|z\|^2\), since the discarded parameter square is nonnegative. Thus the mask is nonexpansive and remains bounded after composition on \(L^2\).





The mask \(M_x\) removes the orthogonal parameter part and keeps the latent part. Its squared norm is exactly the sum of latent coordinate squares, bounded by the full sum. Therefore masking preserves square integrability and continuity of an \(L^2\)-valued input. In particular it produces a latent-only Brownian input whose parameter projection is identically zero.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Average exactly the parameter block}{Average exactly the parameter block}}


Construct the bounded linear map

\[\mathcal M X=\operatorname{const}(I_\theta P_\theta\mathbb E_QX)+M_xX.\]

The expectation estimate and coordinate contractions bound each term by \(\|X\|_2\), so \(\|\mathcal M X\|_2\le2\|X\|_2\). Linearity then proves \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal M)\le2\). This is a conservative sufficient constant.





The averaging operator maps \((\Theta,X)\) to \((\mathbb E\Theta,X)\), with the expected parameter viewed as a constant random variable. Its two terms are bounded linear, so the sum has the stated sufficient norm bound two. This operator averages the parameter score only; it does not replace each latent force by its expectation. That distinction produces the intended interacting parameter–latent mean-field dynamics.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define the actual drift and apply Picard}{Define the actual drift and apply Picard}}


Set \(\mathcal A_V(X)=\mathcal M(\nabla V(X))\). The Nemytskii map for the actual Lipschitz gradient preserves \(L^2\) and has constant \(L\), so \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal A_V)\le2L\). For any continuous \(H\)-valued input \(B\) with \(B_0=0\), and any \(T\ge0\), Picard constructs continuous \(Y\) with \(Y_0=Y^{\rm in}\) and

\[Y_t-Y^{\rm in}=-\int_0^t\mathcal A_V(Y_s)ds+B_t,\qquad0\le t\le T.\]

The proof is an existence theorem in the actual complete Banach space \(H\).




The averaging operator retains the latent gradient pointwise and replaces the parameter gradient by its expectation as a constant random variable. Expectation is a bounded linear map \(L^2\to H\) of norm at most one on a probability space, and the coordinate masks are orthogonal contractions. Thus the averaged drift is globally Lipschitz once the true joint score is Lipschitz. Picard's theorem applies in the complete Hilbert space of square-integrable joint states to a continuous additive input, constructing the actual integral solution on every finite horizon.





A Lipschitz score defines a Lipschitz composition map on \(L^2\) and has affine growth, so it is everywhere defined on the state Hilbert space. Composing with the averaging operator gives constant at most \(2L\). For a continuous additive input, subtracting that input reduces the problem to a standard nonautonomous Lipschitz integral equation. Picard yields a continuous finite-horizon solution and the stated integral identity.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Verify the intended block formulas in L\ensuremath{{}^{2}}}{Verify the intended block formulas in L²}}


Almost everywhere representative equalities show

\[(\mathcal M X)(\omega)=(\mathbb E_Q P_\theta X,\,P_xX(\omega)).\]

Applying parameter projection gives the constant random variable \(\operatorname{const}(P_\theta\mathbb E_QX)\); applying latent projection gives \(P_xX\). The proofs explicitly combine the almost everywhere addition, constant and composition identities, so they are equalities in \(L^2\).





The coordinate formula can be checked on representatives: the included expectation contributes only a constant parameter block, and the mask contributes only the original latent block. Their sum has exactly the displayed components almost surely. Projecting removes the other block without error. These identities show that the abstract averaging construction implements the intended parameter expectation while leaving latent randomness intact.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Identify the parameter force with the true marginal integral}{Identify the parameter force with the true marginal integral}}


Define \(a_V(X)=P_\theta\mathbb E_Q\nabla V(X)\), a continuous map on \(H\). The drift blocks are \(\operatorname{const}(a_V(X))\) and \(P_x\nabla V(X)\). If \(P_\theta X=\operatorname{const}(\theta)\), reconstruct \(X=(\theta,P_xX)\) almost everywhere. Commute the bounded projection through expectation and use the pushforward integral formula to prove

\[a_V(X)=\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\,\mathcal L_Q(P_xX)(dx).\]



If the parameter block is deterministic, the joint state equals \((\theta,P_xX)\) almost surely. Evaluate the actual joint score on this representation, project its parameter block, and commute projection through expectation. Changing variables by the latent pushforward law then gives the marginal integral defining \(b_V\). This law is derived from the current random state, rather than introduced as an independent curve of measures.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Project the integral equation and construct a deterministic parameter}{Project the integral equation and construct a deterministic parameter}}


Suppose the initial parameter is constant \(\theta_0\) and \(P_\theta B_t=0\). Bounded linear maps commute with the Bochner time integral, so projecting the constructed equation gives \(P_\theta Y_t=\operatorname{const}(\theta_t)\), where \(\theta_t=\theta_0-\int_0^t a_V(Y_s)ds\). The continuous primitive proves continuity of \(\theta\). Set \(\mu_t=\mathcal L_Q(P_xY_t)\) and insert the force identity:

\[\theta_t-\theta_0=-\int_0^t b_V(\theta_s,\mu_s)ds,\qquad P_xY_t-P_xY_0=-\int_0^tP_x\nabla V(Y_s)ds+P_xB_t.\]

Both identities hold in their stated deterministic or \(L^2\) sense on the finite horizon.




Project the constructed joint equation onto the parameter and latent blocks. Continuous linear maps commute with the Bochner time integral. Since the additive input has zero parameter block, its parameter coordinate remains a constant random variable, represented by a deterministic continuous path \(\theta_t\). The force identity replaces expectation of the joint parameter score by its integral under the actual latent pushforward law \(\mu_t\), giving \[\theta_t-\theta_0=-\int_0^t\left[\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta_s,x)\,d\mu_s(x)\right]ds.\] The latent projection yields the corresponding drift-plus-noise equation. The parameter law is therefore derived from the constructed process, not introduced as a free auxiliary measure path.





The additive parameter input vanishes and the initial parameter is constant. The projected drift is also constant as a random variable at each time. Hence its time integral is constant, proving the parameter remains deterministic. Its continuous path is defined by the parameter integral equation. Substituting the preceding force identity expresses it in terms of the actual latent law, while latent projection retains the Brownian-driven integral equation.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Construct a continuous latent-\allowbreak{}only Brownian input}{Construct a continuous latent-only Brownian input}}


Apply the latent mask to the actual coordinate Brownian \(L^2\) noise. Brownian second moments prove its continuity in \(L^2\) and its zero value at time zero. Masking makes its parameter projection zero at every time. No separate continuity assumption on a stochastic error process is used.





For the coordinate Brownian vector, \(L^2\) increments have norm proportional to the square root of the time increment, proving \(L^2\) continuity. Multiplication by the bounded latent mask preserves continuity and makes the parameter block zero. Its value at time zero is zero because the Brownian increment from zero to zero vanishes. These facts verify the general input assumptions by construction.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Specialize to Brownian input and to the split envelope}{Specialize to Brownian input and to the split envelope}}


The constructed Brownian input satisfies all hypotheses of the general integral existence theorem, so substitution gives the Brownian-driven \(L^2\) solution. For \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\), the actual proximal calculus supplies the Lipschitz gradient constant \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\), which is included as a derived witness in the conclusion. These results establish continuous \(L^2\) integral equations; they do not assert a pathwise strong-SDE or a Fokker–Planck PDE theorem.





Use the actual latent-only Brownian input in the finite-horizon Picard theorem, then use the proximal calculus to specialize the potential to \(g_1+g_2^\eta\). The sum has gradient constant \(L_1+1/\eta\), so all analytic drift hypotheses have already been proved. The conclusion is an \(L^2\)-valued continuous integral solution with a deterministic parameter equation; stronger pathwise or PDE conclusions require additional arguments.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Exact iid variance and the empirical parameter force}{Exact iid variance and the empirical parameter force}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldVariance-lean}


Independent product copies give an exact Hilbert-valued sample-mean variance formula. Square-integrability justifies expansion of the finite double sum, and independence cancels all off-diagonal terms after centering. Specializing the observable to the actual parameter gradient supplies the quantitative empirical mean-field force fluctuation.




On a probability space, square-integrable Hilbert-valued variables have integrable inner products. On the actual finite product law, independent centered samples yield an exact \(1/N\) variance identity.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Cancel every off-\allowbreak{}diagonal covariance}{Cancel every off-diagonal covariance}}


Realize the supplied \(L^2\) functions as \(L^2\) elements; Cauchy–Schwarz gives integrable pairwise inner products. Expand

\[\mathbb E\left\|\sum_iX_i\right\|^2=\sum_{i,j}\mathbb E\langle X_i,X_j\rangle.\]

For \(i\ne j\), independence factors the expectation into \(\langle\mathbb E X_i,\mathbb E X_j\rangle=0\). The diagonal terms are \(\mathbb E\|X_i\|^2\). Finite sums and established integrability justify the expansion.






For \(i\ne j\), centering gives zero individual means, and independence is what turns the mixed expectation into their zero mean pairing. Centering alone would not remove covariance. The diagonal terms remain the individual variances. Since every product of two \(L^2\) norms is integrable by Cauchy–Schwarz, the finite expansion and its termwise integration are valid ordinary identities.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The exact variance of an independent sample mean}{The exact variance of an independent sample mean}}


For a square-integrable observable \(\phi\) and a probability law \(\mu\), the finite product measure \(\mu^{\otimes N}\) constructs the independent samples. The proof centers \(\phi\), expands the squared Hilbert norm, and uses independence and zero mean to cancel every off-diagonal inner product. The exact result is


\[
\mathbb E_{\mu^{\otimes N}}
 \left\|\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\phi(X_i)-\mathbb E_\mu\phi\right\|^2
 =\frac1N\mathbb E_\mu\|\phi-\mathbb E_\mu\phi\|^2.
\]


Taking square roots proves the \(N^{-1/2}\) sampling factor. The actual parameter-gradient observable is square integrable under finite-P\ensuremath{{}_{2}} latent laws, so the formula applies to the mean-field force.



The source’s exchangeability premise alone does not cancel these covariances. A quantitative chaos estimate needs controlled initialization, such as the actual iid construction used here. This exact sampling identity is a proved ingredient; by itself it is not a propagation-of-chaos theorem.






Let \(C_i=\phi(X_i)-\mathbb E\phi\). The average fluctuation is \(N^{-1}\sum_iC_i\). The sum-square identity gives \(N\mathbb E\|C_1\|^2\), and the prefactor contributes \(N^{-2}\). Their product is exactly \(N^{-1}\mathbb E\|C_1\|^2\). Taking square roots yields the precise sampling factor \(1/\sqrt N\), with iid structure supplied by the actual finite product law.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Specialize the variance to a true gradient observable}{Specialize the variance to a true gradient observable}}


Fix \(\theta\) and let \(\phi(x)=\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)\). A Lipschitz joint gradient makes this observable Lipschitz, hence square integrable under any finite-second-moment latent law \(\mu\). Its expectation is exactly \(b_U(\theta,\mu)\). Substitute into the iid formula to obtain

\[\mathbb E\left\|\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta U(\theta,X_i)-b_U(\theta,\mu)\right\|^2=\frac1N\mathbb E\|\nabla_\theta U(\theta,X)-b_U(\theta,\mu)\|^2.\]




At fixed parameter, the gradient observable is Lipschitz in its latent input and has affine linear growth. A latent law with finite second moment therefore gives finite observable variance. Its expectation is the defined mean-field force. Substitution into the general product identity proves the exact variance formula for the empirical parameter force, supplying a derived fluctuation term rather than a separate variance hypothesis.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Construct the physical particle drift in a coordinate L\ensuremath{{}^{2}} space}{Construct the physical particle drift in a coordinate L² space}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleDrift-lean}


The finite-particle integral equation is constructed on a product of coordinate \(L^2\) spaces with the maximum norm. Each joint pair is rebuilt from the common parameter and one latent variable. The empirical parameter force and individual latent forces then form an actual Lipschitz particle drift with a constant independent of particle count.




Let \(H_N=L^2(P;\Theta)\times\prod_{i<N}L^2(P;X)\) with norm \(\|z\|=\max(\|z_\theta\|_2,\max_i\|z_i\|_2)\). This Banach norm controls every tagged coordinate directly, with no exchangeability hypothesis.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Coordinate inclusions and nonexpansive projections}{Coordinate inclusions and nonexpansive projections}}


Define the coordinate product state and the latent inclusion \(I_x(x)=(0,x)\). The Euclidean square proves \(\|I_xx\|=\|x\|\), while the latent projection has norm at most one. For any bounded linear pointwise contraction \(C\), integrating \(\|C(X)\|^2\le\|X\|^2\) proves \(\|C\circ X\|_2\le\|X\|_2\).





A pointwise linear contraction remains a contraction in \(L^2\) after integrating its squared norm bound. The latent inclusion is an isometry because it adds only zero parameter coordinates. These facts allow parameter and latent random variables to be assembled and projected without hidden dimension constants. The chosen maximum product norm bounds each coordinate error directly.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Assemble one actual joint random pair}{Assemble one actual joint random pair}}


Set \(J(\Theta,X)=I_\theta\Theta+I_xX\). Its representative is the genuine pair \((\Theta(\omega),X(\omega))\) almost everywhere. Linearity of the inclusions and their norm bounds give

\[\|J(\Theta,X)-J(\Theta',X')\|_2\le\|\Theta-\Theta'\|_2+\|X-X'\|_2.\]



The reconstructed pair is the sum of the parameter inclusion and latent inclusion. Their bounded linearity gives the pair-difference bound by the sum of coordinate \(L^2\) differences. In representatives this is exactly \((\Theta(\omega),X(\omega))\) almost surely. Thus evaluating a joint potential or gradient on the assembled variable means evaluating it on the intended actual parameter–latent pair.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Define the gradient and empirical particle drift}{Define the gradient and empirical particle drift}}


Apply the actual gradient \(\nabla V\) to each pair \(J(\Theta,X_i)\). The parameter force is the empirical average and each latent force is its own gradient block:

\[\mathcal A_N(z)=\left(\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta V(\Theta,X_i),\ (\nabla_xV(\Theta,X_i))_i\right).\]

Almost everywhere composition and finite-sum identities verify these exact coordinate formulas. The definition does not replace the empirical parameter force by a mean-field expectation.
